On the semi-additivity of the 1 / 2 1/2 -symmetric caloric capacity Thanks: J. H. has been supported by PID2020-114167GB-I00 (Mineco, Spain). J. M. has been partially supported by 2021SGR-00071 (Catalonia) and PID2020-112881GB-I00 (Mineco, Spain). L.P. has been partially supported by 2021SGR-00071 (Catalonia) and PID2020-114167GB-I00 (Mineco, Spain)
Joan Hernández, Joan Mateu, and Laura Prat

Abstract
. In this paper we study properties of a variant of the 1 / 2 1/2 -caloric capacity, called 1 / 2 1/2 -symmetric caloric capacity. The latter is associated simultaneously with the 1 / 2 1/2 -fractional heat equation and its conjugate. We establish its semi-additivity in ℝ n + 1 \mathbb{R}^{n+1} and, moreover, we compute explicitly the 1 / 2 1/2 -symmetric caloric capacity of rectangles, which illustrates its anisotropic behavior. AMS 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 42B20 (primary); 28A12 (secondary). Keywords: Fractional heat equation, singular integrals, caloric capacity.



1.
Introduction

The study of removable subsets for caloric equations done by Mateu, Prat & Tolsa in
[
MPrTo
]
and by Mateu & Prat in
[
MPr
]
introduces, naturally, caloric capacities associated to such PDE’s. In the first reference
(1,1/2)
-Lipschitz caloric capacity was introduced, as well as the notion of equivalence between the nullity of this quantity and the removability of compact subsets for the heat equation, i.e. the one associated with the differential operator
\Theta:=(-\Delta_{x})+\partial_{t}
, where
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. In
[
MPr
]
, Mateu & Prat studied the corresponding caloric capacities associated with the fractional heat equation. That is, the equation associated with the pseudo-differential operator
\Theta^{s}:=(-\Delta_{x})^{s}+\partial_{t}
, for
0<s<1
. The authors distinguished the cases
s=1/2
,
1/2<s<1
and
0<s<1/2
, focusing mainly on the first. The study for
s=1/2
was quite successful, obtaining a removability result for the
\Theta^{1/2}
-equation, as it was done for the heat equation. For instance, if
f
is a solution of the
1/2
-heat equation in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{E}
satisfying
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}<\infty,
then
E
will be removable if and only if the
1/2
-caloric capacity of
E
is null. One of the main features that characterized the case
s=1/2
is that the authors could work with an explicit fundamental solution for the operator
\Theta^{1/2}
, namely

P(x,t):=\frac{t}{\big(|x|^{2}+t^{2}\big)^{(n+1)/2}}\chi_{\{t>0\}}(x,t),

a continuous function in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{\{0\}}
, harmonic in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{\{t=0\}}
. This, together with the fact that the Fourier symbols of
(-\Delta_{x})^{1/2}
and
\partial_{t}
share the same homogeneity, allowed Mateu & Prat to characterize the removability of compact sets in a simpler way than the cases
s\neq 1/2
. More precisely, they established that the removability condition for
E
can be studied by controlling the supremum of expressions of the form
|\langle T,1\rangle|
, where
T
is a distribution supported on
E
satisfying the normalization condition
\|P\ast T\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq 1
. The latter supremum is known as the
1/2
-caloric capacity of
E
, written
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2}}(E)
. If the supremum is only taken among positive Borel measures supported on
E
(with the same normalization condition), we obtain the smaller capacity
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2},+}(E)
. On the other hand, for
s\neq 1/2
, although there is a fundamental solution
P_{s}(x,t)
, its expression is no longer explicit. However, there are precise estimates computed by the probabilists Blumenthal & Getoor
[
BlGe
]
who described the power-like tail behaviour of
P_{s}
and allowed the authors to carry out a similar study. Nevertheless, there was still a technical obstacle: the fact that the Fourier symbols of
(-\Delta_{x})^{s}
and
\partial_{t}
do not share homogeneity. This feature, essentially, ended up by limiting the amount of results obtained.

In the present paper we aim at obtaining a more precise description of a smaller variant of
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2}}
, already introduced in
[
MPr
, §4]
. Denoted by
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2}}
, we call it
1/2
-
symmetric caloric capacity
and it is the supremum of expressions of the form
|\langle T,1\rangle|
, where
T
is a distribution supported on a compact set now satisfying both conditions

\|P\ast T\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq 1\quad\text{ and }\quad\|P^{\ast}\ast T\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq 1,

where
P^{\ast}(x,t):=P(-x,-t)
is the conjugate kernel of
P
. We will write
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2},+}
its smaller version, which takes into account only positive Borel measures. The main result of this text is found in Section
6
and it states that, in fact, the latter capacity is not that small:

Theorem
.

For
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
compact,

\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2}}(E)\approx\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2},+}(E).

Such result implies, bearing in mind the results of
[
He
]
, the semi-additivity of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2}}
(Theorem
6.7
). Sections
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
are devoted to present the notation and preliminary results necessary to prove the above estimates. In Section
2
we introduce basic terminology and properties, in Section
3
we prove Theorem
3.5
, which encapsulates all the different ways to define, equivalently,
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2},+}
. In Section
4
we use a particular characterization of the latter via a variational approach. With it, we construct a Whitney decomposition of a certain family of compact sets, so that we gain control of their
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2}}
capacity. Finally, in Section
5
, we prove Theorem
5.19
, a general comparability result between
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2}}
and
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2}}
if an additional assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is satisfied, and in Section
6
we remove such assumption and obtain the general result. Our methods are influenced by those of Tolsa
[
To3
, Ch.5]
and Volberg
[
Vo
]
, where the same type of comparability results are studied for analytic and Lipschitz harmonic capacity respectively.

In our setting, since
P
is not an anti-symmetric kernel, such arguments can be applied with some additional assumptions based on those of general
Tb
-theorems (see
[
NTVo2
]
and
[
HyMar
]
). The fact that
P
is harmonic outside the hyperplane
\{t=0\}
, an
\pazocal{L}^{n+1}
-null set, and that satisfies being an
n
-dimensional Calderón-Zygmund kernel
[
MPr
, Lemma 2.1]
are the essential features used to carry out similar arguments. Finally, Section
7
is devoted to the computation of the
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2}}
capacity of a rectangle
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, obtaining:

\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2}}(R)\approx\ell_{t}\,\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{\ell_{t}^{2}}{\ell_{x}^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{\ell_{t}}{\ell_{x}}\arctan\bigg(\frac{\ell_{x}}{\ell_{t}}\bigg)\Bigg]^{-1},

where
\ell_{x}
and
\ell_{t}
are the horizontal and vertical side lengths of
R
. The above behavior differs substantially, for example, with that obtained for
\gamma(R)
, the analytic capacity of a rectangle. For the latter one has
\gamma(R)\approx\text{diam}(R)
(see
[
To3
, Proposition 1.5]
).

Let us also stress one last feature of the above capacities associated to the
1/2
-fractional heat equations: the kernels
P
and
P^{\ast}
are both
nonnegative
. This suggests that, maybe, using classical arguments of potential theory (see
[
La
, Ch.I & II]
,
[
Ra
, Ch.3]
or
[
Ki
]
), the comparability between capacities defined through positive measures and distributions should follow by a simpler argument similar to that of
[
Ve
, p.10]
, provided that one is able to prove the existence of an
equilibrium measure
. The authors have not been able to deduce its existence for
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2},+}
, the main obstacle being the incapability of obtaining an equivalent formulation of the latter capacity in terms of an (inverted) infimum over energies. The following simple example already illustrates some problems that arise by proceeding this way: pick
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
the horizontal line segment
[0,1]\times\{0\}
and
\mu:=\pazocal{H}^{1}|_{E}
. In
[
MPr
, §6]
Mateu & Prat bound explicitly
P\ast\mu
by
\pi
, obtaining that
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2},+}(E)\geq\pi^{-1}
. However, by the definition of
P
and the choice of
\mu
, it is also clear that
P\ast\mu(x,0)=0
, for any
x\in[0,1]
. Therefore, if we were to compute the energy of
\mu
we would obtain

I[\mu]:=\int P\ast\mu\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{0}^{1}P\ast\mu(x,0)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\pazocal{H}^{1}(x)=0,

which would imply
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2},+}(E)=+\infty
. The latter example also shows that the potentials
P\ast\mu
do not obey the so called
maximum principle
, i.e. they do not attain their maximum values at the
\text{supp}(\mu)
. To avoid this problem, the authors have tried to work with the auxiliary potentials
U_{\mu}(x,t):=\limsup_{(y,s)\to(x,t)}P\ast\mu(y,s)
. For these the maximum principle holds (this follows, essentially, from the fact that
P
is subharmonic in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{\{0\}}
). However, the potentials
U_{\mu}
still lack the
continuity principle
, i.e. if
U_{\mu}
restricted to
\text{supp}(\mu)
is continuous, then
U_{\mu}
should be continuous everywhere else (it already fails in the same example of above); which is an essential tool in order to carry out the construction of possible equilibrium measures. In any case, the above classical methods were finally discarded and we chose to follow arguments similar to those of
[
To3
, Ch.5]
and
[
Vo
]
.

About the notation used in the sequel
: We use an upper bar to denote generic points in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, as for example
\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z}\ldots
For a given
\overline{x}=(x,t)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
will be usually referred to as the
spatial
variable, and
t\in\mathbb{R}
the
time
variable.

Constants appearing in this text can depend on the dimension of the ambient space and possibly on the Calderón-Zygmund (CZ) constants of
P
, and their value may change at different occurrences. The notation
A\lesssim B
means that there exists such a constant
C
, so that
A\leq CB
. Moreover,
A\approx B
is equivalent to
A\lesssim B\lesssim A
. Also,
A\simeq B
will mean
A=CB
.

We will write
B_{r}(\overline{x})
to denote the usual Euclidean ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
of radius
r>0
and center
\overline{x}
. A cube in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
centered at
\overline{x}
with side length
\ell(Q)
will be denoted
Q(\overline{x},\ell(Q))
and usually named
Q,R,S\ldots
Cubes will always have sides parallel to the coordinate axes. If we write
\lambda Q
we mean a dilation of
Q
of factor
\lambda
. That is,
\lambda Q
will be the cube concentric with
Q
of side length
\lambda\ell(Q)
(we follow analogous conventions with balls).

We also emphasize that the gradient symbol
\nabla
will refer to
(\nabla_{x},\partial_{t})
, with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

We will also write
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}:=\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}
.

The symbol
\chi_{A}
, where
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is Borel measurable, means the indicator function that equals
1
on
A
, and is 0 otherwise.

For any function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
, the symbol
f^{\ast}
will denote
f^{\ast}(\overline{x}):=f(-\overline{x})
. Hence
f
being an even function is equivalent to
f=f^{\ast}
.

Fourier transforms of smooth functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
will be denoted by
\pazocal{F}[f]
or simply as
\widehat{f}
. They will always be taken with respect to spatial variables. For a fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we put
g_{t}(x):=f(x,t)
and write
\widehat{f}(\cdot,t):=\widehat{g_{t}}
.

Since the Laplacian operator (fractional or not) will frequently appear in our discussion and will always be taken with respect to spatial variables, we will adopt the notation
\Delta:=\Delta_{x}
.

2.
Notation and basic definitions

Our ambient space will be
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
\Theta^{1/2}
will denote the
1/2
-heat operator
,

\Theta^{1/2}:=(-\Delta)^{1/2}+\partial_{t},

where
(-\Delta)^{1/2}
is a pseudo-differential operator known as the
1/2
-Laplacian
with respect to the spatial variable. It may be defined through its Fourier transform for every fixed
t
,

\pazocal{F}[{(-\Delta)^{1/2}}f](\xi,t)=|\xi|\pazocal{F}[f](\xi,t),

or by its integral representation

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{1/2}f(x,t)

\displaystyle\simeq\text{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{f(x,t)-f(y,t)}{|x-y|^{n+1}}\text{d}y

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{f(x+y,t)-2f(x,t)+f(x-y,t)}{|y|^{n+1}}\text{d}y.

The reader may find more details about the properties of such operator in
[
DPV
, §3]
or
[
St
]
. Borrowing the notation of
[
MPr
]
, let
P
be the fundamental solution of the
1/2
-heat equation in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, which is given by
[
Va
, Eq. 2.2]

P(\overline{x})=\frac{t}{|\overline{x}|^{n+1}}\,\chi_{\{t>0\}}(\overline{x}).

Notice that the previous kernel is locally integrable in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, continuous in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{\{0\}}
and not differentiable at any point of the hyperplane
\{t=0\}
. Another function that will frequently appear is
P^{\ast}
,

P^{\ast}(\overline{x}):=P(-\overline{x})=\frac{-t}{|\overline{x}|^{n+1}}\,\chi_{\{t<0\}}(\overline{x}),

that is the conjugate kernel associated to
P
. For a given distribution
T
, we also define
T^{\ast}
to be the distribution acting as

\langle T^{\ast},\varphi\rangle:=\langle T,\varphi^{\ast}\rangle,\quad\forall\varphi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}).

It is not hard to check (approximating via test functions and using the associativity of
[
Ca
, Theorem 8.15]
) that for any
T
distribution and
S
distribution with compact support,

(2.1)

\langle T\ast S,\varphi\rangle=\langle S,T^{\ast}\ast\varphi\rangle,\quad\forall\varphi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}).

With this notions we observe that on the one hand, for any
\overline{x}=(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
we have

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{1/2}

\displaystyle P^{\ast}(\overline{x})=C^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{P^{\ast}(x+y,t)-2P^{\ast}(x,t)+P^{\ast}(x-y,t)}{|y|^{n+1}}\text{d}y

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt=C^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{P(-x-y,-t)-2P(-x,-t)+P(-x+y,-t)}{|y|^{n+1}}\text{d}y=(-\Delta)^{1/2}P(-\overline{x}).

That is, we have

(-\Delta)^{1/2}P^{\ast}=\big[(-\Delta)^{1/2}P\big]^{\ast}.

On the other hand, we also have for any
\varphi
test function in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

\langle(\partial_{t}P)^{\ast},\varphi\rangle:=\langle\partial_{t}P,\varphi^{\ast}\rangle:=-\langle P,\partial_{t}\varphi^{\ast}\rangle=\langle P,(\partial_{t}\varphi)^{\ast}\rangle=\langle P^{\ast},\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle=:-\langle\partial_{t}P^{\ast},\varphi\rangle.

Therefore, the following distributional identity holds

(\partial_{t}P)^{\ast}=-\partial_{t}P^{\ast}.

Hence, if we define the operator

\overline{\Theta}^{1/2}:=(-\Delta)^{1/2}-\partial_{t},

we have that

\overline{\Theta}^{1/2}P^{\ast}=\big[\Theta^{1/2}P\big]^{\ast}=\delta_{0}^{\ast}=\delta_{0},

implying that
P^{\ast}
is the fundamental solution of
\overline{\Theta}^{1/2}
. We will refer to
P^{\ast}
and
\overline{\Theta}^{1/2}
as the
conjugates
of
P
and
\Theta^{1/2}
respectively.

We recall the definition of the
1/2
-caloric capacity
and some of its fundamental variants introduced in
[
MPr
]
. For
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
compact, define its
1/2
-caloric capacity as

\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2}}(E)=\sup|\langle T,1\rangle|,

where the supremum is taken over all distributions
T
with
\text{supp}(T)\subset E
satisfying

\|P\ast T\|_{\infty}:=\|P\ast T\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq 1.

Such distributions will be called
admissible for
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2}}(E)
. In
[
MPr
, Lemma 3.4]
it is proved that for a distribution
T
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
\|P\ast T\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, if
\varphi
is a test function supported on
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
a cube, with
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\leq\ell(Q)^{-1}
, then

|\langle T,\varphi\rangle|\leq C\ell(Q)^{n},

for some constant
C>0
, that we may assume
C\geq 1
without loss of generality.

Definition 2.1
.

If the previous property holds for a distribution
T
, we say that
T
has
growth
of degree
n
or simply
n
-growth (
with constant
C
). We write
\pazocal{T}_{n}(E)
those distributions with
n
-growth and constant 1 supported on a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

It is easy to see that the previous definition of growth for a distribution generalizes the usual notion of growth if
T
coincides with a positive measure.

Definition 2.2
.

Recall that a positive measure
\mu
is said to have
n
-growth with constant
C
if
\mu\big(B(\overline{x},r)\big)\leq Cr^{n}
, for all
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\,r>0
. We also write
\Sigma_{n}(E)
the collection measures with
n
-growth and constant 1 supported on a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

It is clear that the above properties are invariant if formulated using cubes instead of balls.

The fact that
C
depends only on the dimension of the ambient space and the CZ constants of
P
, motivates the following redefinition of
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2}}
, that will be the one used throughout the whole text:

Definition 2.3
.

For a compact subset
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, define its
1/2
-caloric capacity
as

\gamma(E)=\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2}}(E):=\sup|\langle T,1\rangle|,

the supremum taken over distributions in
\pazocal{T}_{n}(E)
and satisfying
\|P\ast T\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.

We also define the (
1/2,+
)-
caloric capacity
, denoted by
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2},+}
, in the same way as
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2}}
, but with the supremum only taken with respect to positive Borel measures. More precisely,

\gamma_{+}(E)=\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2},+}(E):=\sup\big\{\mu(E)\,:\,\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E),\,\|P\ast\mu\|_{\infty}\leq 1\big\},

Analogous definitions are associated with the operator
\overline{\Theta}^{1/2}
, giving rise to the objects

\overline{\gamma}=\gamma_{\overline{\Theta}^{1/2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\gamma}_{+}=\gamma_{\overline{\Theta}^{1/2},+}.

Moreover, it is usual to extend all of the above definitions to a greater variety of sets. Namely, if
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is any Borel set,

\gamma(E):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\subseteq E\\
K\text{compact}\end{subarray}}\gamma(K),

and similarly for the rest of capacities.

In some of the results presented in
[
MPr
, §3]
, one encounters expressions of the form
\langle T,\varphi\rangle
, where
\varphi
is a compactly supported
\pazocal{C}^{1}
function, and
T
is a compactly supported distribution satisfying
\|P\ast T\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. This last estimate and the fact that
(-\Delta)^{1/2}\varphi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, proved in
[
MPr
, §3]
, allows to give meaning to
\langle T,\varphi\rangle
(that a priori may not have sense since
\varphi
is not a test function) as follows:

\langle T,\varphi\rangle:=\big\langle P\ast T,\overline{\Theta}^{1/2}\varphi\big\rangle=\big\langle P\ast T,(-\Delta)^{1/2}\varphi+\partial_{t}\varphi\big\rangle.

We need to extend such definition to a slightly wider class of functions. We do not claim that such class will be the largest where
\langle T,\varphi\rangle
can be defined if
T
is a compactly supported distribution with
\|P\ast T\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, but it will suffice for our purposes.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a cube,
\pazocal{N}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
a
\pazocal{L}^{n+1}
-null set, and
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
a function. For
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
we will write

\varphi\in\pazocal{C}_{c}(Q)\cap\Big(\pazocal{C}_{x}^{m}(Q),\pazocal{C}_{t}^{m}(Q\setminus{\pazocal{N}})\Big)\quad\text{or simply}\quad\varphi\in\pazocal{C}^{m}_{c,\pazocal{N}}(Q),

if
\varphi
is continuous and compactly supported on
Q
,
m
times continuously differentiable with respect to the spatial variables, and
m
times continuously differentiable with respect to the temporal variable
t
except for a null set
\pazocal{N}
(where it may not even be differentiable). We also write

\varphi\in\pazocal{C}(Q)\cap\Big(\pazocal{C}_{x}^{m}(Q),\pazocal{C}_{t}^{m}(Q\setminus{\pazocal{N}})\Big)=\pazocal{C}^{m}_{\pazocal{N}}(Q),

if
\varphi
satisfies the above regularity properties on
Q
, but we do not require it to be compactly supported there (in fact, it may even have a larger domain of definition). In the sequel we will only be interested in the case
m=\infty
.

For
\varphi\in\pazocal{C}^{m}_{c,\pazocal{N}}(Q)
one can define
\langle T,\varphi\rangle
as above, assuming
\|P\ast T\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
T
compactly supported. Indeed, observe that we only have to give meaning to
\langle P\ast T,(-\Delta)^{1/2}\varphi\rangle
and
\langle P\ast T,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle
. The former term can be defined as in
[
MPr
, §3]
, where the integrability of
(-\Delta)^{1/2}\varphi
is proved using only regularity assumptions over the spatial variables. Regarding the term
\langle P\ast T,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle
, since
P\ast T\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, it can be simply defined as

\langle P\ast T,\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle:=\int_{\text{supp}(\varphi)\setminus{\pazocal{N}}}P\ast T(\overline{x})\partial_{t}\varphi(\overline{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{x}.

Remark
2.1
.

We notice that if for
\varphi\in\pazocal{C}_{c,\pazocal{N}}^{1}(Q)
it also happens that
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\leq\ell(Q)^{-1}
, then
[
MPr
, Corollary 3.3]
also holds with exactly the same proof for such
\varphi
; and therefore also does
[
MPr
, Theorem 3.1]
(localization of potentials) and
[
MPr
, Lemma 3.4]
(
n
-growth of admissible distributions). Here, condition
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\leq\ell(Q)^{-1}
must be understood as: for every test function
\psi
,

|\langle\nabla\varphi,\psi\rangle|=\bigg\rvert\int_{Q}\varphi(\nabla\psi)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\pazocal{L}^{n+1}\bigg\rvert=\bigg\rvert\int_{Q\setminus{\pazocal{{N}}}}(\nabla\varphi)\psi\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\pazocal{L}^{n+1}\bigg\rvert\leq\ell(Q)^{-1}\|\psi\|_{L^{1}}.

To end this introductory section, we define more capacities that are already presented in
[
MPr
]
and some other auxiliary ones. First, the so called 1/2-
symmetric caloric capacities
, where the normalization conditions for the potentials against
P
and
P^{\ast}
are both required:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}(E)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2}}(E)

\displaystyle:=\sup\big\{|\langle T,1\rangle|\,:\,T\in\pazocal{T}_{n}(E),\,\|P\ast T\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\|P^{\ast}\ast T\|_{\infty}\leq 1\big\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{\Theta^{1/2},+}(E)

\displaystyle:=\sup\big\{\mu(E)\,:\,\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E),\,\|P\ast\mu\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\|P^{\ast}\ast\mu\|_{\infty}\leq 1\big\}.

We also have the following auxiliary capacities, that we only consider in the context of positive measures:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\text{{sy}},+}(E)

\displaystyle:=\sup\big\{\mu(E)\,:\,\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E),\,\|P_{\text{{sy}}}\ast\mu\|_{\infty}\leq 1\big\},

where
P_{\text{{sy}}}
is the symmetric part of
P
, that is,

P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}):=\frac{P(\overline{x})+P(-\overline{x})}{2}=\frac{|t|}{2|\overline{x}|^{n+1}}.

Finally, we also consider the following capacities defined via a normalization condition that involves an
L^{2}(\mu)
operator bound:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\text{op}}(E)=\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2},\text{op}}(E)

\displaystyle:=\sup\big\{\mu(E)\;:\;\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E),\;\|\pazocal{P}_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq 1\big\},

\displaystyle\gamma_{\text{sy},\text{op}}(E)

\displaystyle:=\sup\big\{\mu(E)\;:\;\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E),\;\|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq 1\big\},

where
\pazocal{P}_{\mu}
and
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\mu}
are the convolution operators associated to
P
and
P_{\text{{sy}}}
with respect to the measure
\mu
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, we will also denote by
\pazocal{P}_{\mu,\varepsilon}
and
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\mu,\varepsilon}
the convolution operators associated to the truncated kernels
P(\overline{x})\chi_{\{|\overline{x}|>\varepsilon\}}
and
P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x})\chi_{\{|\overline{x}|>\varepsilon\}}
respectively. With this definition, we will understand

\|\pazocal{P}_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}:=\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\|\pazocal{P}_{\mu,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)},

and analogously for
P_{\text{{sy}}}
. We will frequently use the notation:

\pazocal{P}(f\mu):=\pazocal{P}_{\mu}f:=P\ast(f\mu),

and normally
f
will happen to be the constant function
1
, so the reader has to understand
\pazocal{P}\mu
as
\pazocal{P}_{\mu}1
. The same considerations will be done for
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy}}
and for
\pazocal{P}^{\ast}
, the latter being the convolution operator associated to the conjugate kernel
P^{\ast}
.

From the results of
[
MPr
]
and
[
He
]
one can deduce an important property of the above different capacities: most of them are comparable.

Theorem 2.1
.

The previous capacities defined through positive measures are all comparable. That is, for any compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}(E)\approx\overline{\gamma}_{+}(E)\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)

\displaystyle\approx\gamma_{\text{{sy}},+}(E)

\displaystyle\approx\gamma_{\text{{op}}}(E)\approx\gamma_{\text{{sy,op}}}(E).

Remark
2.2
.

To be precise, the capacity
\gamma_{\text{{sy,op}}}
is not introduced in the aforementioned references. To deduce the comparability of
\gamma_{\text{{sy,op}}}
to all the rest it suffices to check
\gamma_{\text{sy,op}}\lesssim\gamma_{\text{op}}
, since the reverse inequality can be verified to hold easily. Fix
\mu
any admissible measure for
\gamma_{\text{{sy,op}}}(E)
with
\gamma_{\text{{sy,op}}}(E)\leq 2\mu(E)
. Notice that since
\pazocal{P}_{\mu}
and
\pazocal{P}^{\ast}_{\mu}
are
n
-dimensional CZ operators, then
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\mu}
also satisfies such property. Therefore, following an analogous proof to
[
To3
, Theorem 2.16]
one deduces, from the
L^{2}(\mu)
-boundedness of
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\mu}
, its boundedness as an operator from the space of Borel finite signed measures
M(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
to
L^{1,\infty}(\mu)
. That is, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
\nu\in M(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
,

\mu\big(\big\{|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\nu(\overline{x})|>\lambda\big\}\big)\leq C\frac{\|\nu\|}{\lambda},\qquad\forall\varepsilon>0,\,\forall\lambda>0.

With this, and using that
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy}}=\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy}}^{\ast}
(the operator is its own adjoint), applying for example
[
Ch
, Ch.VII, Theorem 23]
, we can find a function
h:E\to[0,1]
such that
\int_{E}h\text{d}\mu\geq\mu(E)/2
and
\|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy}}(h\mu)\|_{\infty}<4C
. Then,

\gamma_{\text{sy},+}(E)\geq\frac{1}{4C}\int_{E}h\text{d}\mu\geq\frac{\mu(E)}{8C}\geq\frac{1}{16C}\,\gamma_{\text{sy,op}}(E),

that implies
\gamma_{\text{sy,op}}(E)\lesssim\gamma_{\text{sy},+}(E)\approx\gamma_{\text{op}}(E)
, and we are done.

3.
Even more capacities

The main goal of this section is to add three additional ways of understanding
1/2
-caloric capacities: one via an
L^{2}(\mu)
normalization condition, another using a uniform bound at
all
points of the compact set, and a last one formulated in a variational way. All the results proved in this section are summarized in Theorem
3.5
.

3.1.
L^{2}(\mu)
normalization. Suppressed kernels.

Given a convolution kernel one may define its
suppressed version
in terms of a general nonnegative 1-Lipschitz function (with constant 1). However, for our purposes we do not need to work with such generality, and we will focus on a particular type of functions. Given a closed set
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we will typically have

\Lambda(\overline{x}):=\text{dist}(\overline{x},F),\quad\forall\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

The above
\Lambda
is indeed a 1-Lipschitz function: take any
\overline{x},\overline{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and observe that for any
\overline{z}\in F
we have
\Lambda(\overline{x})\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{z}|\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|+|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|
. Since
\overline{z}
is arbitrary,
\Lambda(\overline{x})\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|+\Lambda(\overline{y})\,\leftrightarrow\,\Lambda(\overline{x})-\Lambda(\overline{y})\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|
. Repeating the same argument changing the roles of
\overline{x}
and
\overline{y}
we deduce the desired Lipschitz property. We define the suppressed kernel associated to
P
, that depends on
\Lambda
and a previously fixed closed set
F
, as:

(3.1)

P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y}):=\frac{P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})}{1+P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{2}\Lambda(\overline{x})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}},

for all the pair of points where such expression makes sense. Notice that it is also a nonnegative kernel and differentiable, with respect to
\overline{x}
or
\overline{y}
, out of a set of null Lebesgue measure. We may also define
P_{\Lambda}^{\ast}
and
P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}
in an analogous way. In fact, for the sake of notation, we shall prove three basic properties only for
P_{\Lambda}
, but they can be checked to hold also for
P_{\Lambda}^{\ast}
and
P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}
with almost exactly the same proofs. Essentially the convolution kernels
P_{\Lambda},P_{\Lambda}^{\ast}
and
P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
define
n
-dimensional CZ operators. In fact, such suppressed kernels satisfy
3
as an additional property.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{[}0,\infty)
be a 1-Lipschitz function. Then, kernels
P_{\Lambda},P_{\Lambda}^{\ast}
and
P_{\text{{sy}},\Lambda}
define
n
-dimensional CZ operators which satisfy being “well suppressed” at the points where
\Lambda>0
. Namely, if we fix any
\overline{x}\neq\overline{y}
points in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, the precise estimates that
P_{\Lambda}
satisfies
(
as well as
P_{\Lambda}^{\ast}
and
P_{\text{{sy}},\Lambda})
are

1
.

|P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})|\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{-n}
.

2
.

For any
\overline{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
satisfying
|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|/2
,

\big\rvert P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})-P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})\big\rvert\lesssim\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}\quad\text{and}\quad\big\rvert P_{\Lambda}(\overline{y},\overline{x})-P_{\Lambda}(\overline{y},\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert\lesssim\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}.

In particular, where the differentiation is well-defined,

\big\rvert\nabla_{\overline{x}}P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\big\rvert+\big\rvert\nabla_{\overline{y}}P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\big\rvert\lesssim\frac{1}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}.

3
.

|P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})|\lesssim\min\big\{\Lambda(\overline{x})^{-n},\Lambda(\overline{y})^{-n}\big\}
.

Proof.

The proof of
1
is trivial, since
|P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})|\leq P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})
. We move on by proving the first inequality in
2
, and we will do it in a way that the arguments will also be valid for
P^{\ast}
, implying the validity of the second estimate of
2
. Let us first assume
\Lambda(\overline{x}^{\prime})\leq\Lambda(\overline{x})
and define

g(\overline{x},\overline{y}):=1+P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{2}\Lambda(\overline{x})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}.

Using
[
MPr
, Lemma 2.1]
(that is also valid for
P^{\ast}
and hence also for
P_{\text{{sy}}}
) we get

\displaystyle|P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},

\displaystyle\overline{y})-P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})-P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})|}{g(\overline{x},\overline{y})}+P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})\frac{|P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{2}\Lambda(\overline{x})^{n}-P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})^{2}\Lambda(\overline{x}^{\prime})^{n}|\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}{g(\overline{x},\overline{y})g(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}+P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})\frac{|P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{2}-P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})^{2}|\Lambda(\overline{x}^{\prime})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}{g(\overline{x},\overline{y})g(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})\frac{P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{2}|\Lambda(\overline{x})^{n}-\Lambda(\overline{x}^{\prime})^{n}|\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}{g(\overline{x},\overline{y})g(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})}=:\text{I}+\text{II}+\text{III}.

We only have to deal with II and III. For the first, we rewrite it as

\text{II}=|P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})-P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})|\cdot\text{IV},

where

\text{IV}:=\frac{P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})|P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})+P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})|\Lambda(\overline{x}^{\prime})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}{g(\overline{x},\overline{y})g(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})}.

It is clear that by the definition of
g

\text{IV}\leq 1+\frac{P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\Lambda(\overline{x}^{\prime})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}{g(\overline{x},\overline{y})g(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})}.

For the remaining fraction, if
P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\leq P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})
we obtain the same bound by 1. If on the other hand
P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})>P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})
, using that
\Lambda(\overline{x}^{\prime})\leq\Lambda(\overline{x})
we also obtain the same estimate and we are done with II. Regarding III, observe that there exists a point
\overline{\xi}
in the line segment joining
\overline{x}
and
\overline{x}^{\prime}
such that

\text{III}\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})\frac{n\Lambda(\overline{\xi})^{n-1}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{2}}{g(\overline{x},\overline{y})g(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})}.

We distinguish two cases: if
\Lambda(\overline{\xi})\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|
. By the Lipschitz property of
\Lambda
,
\Lambda(\overline{y})\leq 2|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|
. Thus,

III

\displaystyle\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|\frac{n\Lambda(\overline{\xi})^{n-1}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{2}}{g(\overline{x},\overline{y})g(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})}\Big[P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})+\big\rvert P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})-P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})\big\rvert\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{2n-1}}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{2n}}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n}}+\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}\Bigg]=\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}\Bigg[1+\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}},

that is the desired estimate. If on the other hand we had
\Lambda(\overline{\xi})>|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|
, then notice that by the Lipschitz property of
\Lambda
,

\Lambda(\overline{\xi})\leq\Lambda(\overline{x})+|\overline{x}-\overline{\xi}|\leq\Lambda(\overline{x})+|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|\leq\Lambda(\overline{x})+\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|}{2}<\Lambda(\overline{x})+\frac{\Lambda(\overline{\xi})}{2},

that is
\Lambda(\overline{x})>\Lambda(\overline{\xi})/2
, and in particular
g(\overline{x},\overline{y})\gtrsim g(\overline{\xi},\overline{y})
. Therefore,

III

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|}\frac{P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{2}\Lambda(\overline{\xi})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}{g(\overline{\xi},\overline{y})g(\overline{x}^{\prime},\overline{y})}\leq P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\big\rvert P(\overline{x}^{\prime}-\overline{y})-P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\big\rvert+\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}\lesssim\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}},

that is what we wanted to prove. With this, we have proved
2
for the case
\Lambda(\overline{x}^{\prime})\leq\Lambda(\overline{x})
. The case
\Lambda(\overline{x})\leq\Lambda(\overline{x}^{\prime})
can be treated analogously, using symmetric considerations with respect to
\overline{x}
and
\overline{x}^{\prime}
.

To prove the gradient estimates, let us fix
j\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
and consider
\partial_{x_{j}}P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})
at the points where this expression is well defined. Observe that since
\Lambda
is a Lipschitz function and
P
can be differentiated except for null Lebesgue sets,
\partial_{x_{j}}P_{\Lambda}
makes sense out of sets of null Lebesgue measure. In any case, choose
0<|h|\ll 1
so that
|h|\leq|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|/2
, where
|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|
is strictly positive since
\overline{x}\neq\overline{y}
are two fixed different points. Hence, applying the already proved estimates of
2
we get

\frac{|P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x}+h\widehat{e}_{j},\overline{y})-P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})|}{|h|}\lesssim\frac{1}{|h|}\frac{|h|}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}=\frac{1}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}},

so taking the limit as
h\to 0
the result follows.

Finally we prove
3
. Assume that
\Lambda(\overline{x})=\max\big\{\Lambda(\overline{x}),\Lambda(\overline{y})\big\}
. In this setting,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\big\rvert P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\big\rvert=\frac{1}{P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})}\Bigg[\frac{1}{P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{-2}+\Lambda(\overline{x})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}\Bigg]\leq\frac{1}{P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})}\frac{1}{\Lambda(\overline{x})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}.

Assume that

(3.3)

\frac{1}{P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})}\leq\frac{\Lambda(\overline{x})^{n}}{2^{n}}.

In this case,
|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n}\leq P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})^{-1}\leq\Lambda(\overline{x})^{n}/2^{n}
, that is
|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|\leq\Lambda(\overline{x})/2
. By the Lipschitz property we also have
\Lambda(\overline{y})\geq\Lambda(\overline{x})-|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|
, so
\Lambda(\overline{y})\geq\Lambda(\overline{x})/2
, and applying this estimate to (
3.2
) the result would follow. If (
3.3
) did not hold, we would simply have

|P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})|\leq|P(\overline{x}-\overline{y})|\leq\frac{2^{n}}{\Lambda(\overline{x})^{n}},

and we would be also done. Moreover, it is clear that if
\Lambda(\overline{y})=\max\big\{\Lambda(\overline{x}),\Lambda(\overline{y})\big\}
, the above proof can be carried out in an analogous way, obtaining the desired result.
∎

For a fixed
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
compact set, we will use the above suppressed kernels to study the capacity

\gamma_{\text{sy},2}(E):=\sup\big\{\mu(E)\,:\,\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E),\,\|P_{\text{{sy}}}\ast\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\mu(E)^{1/2}\big\}.

It is clear that
\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\text{{op}}}(E)\leq\gamma_{\text{sy},2}(E)
. In the following lemma we prove that the reverse inequality also holds:

Lemma 3.2
.

For
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
compact, the following inequality holds:

\gamma_{\text{{sy}},2}(E)\lesssim\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\text{{op}}}(E).

Proof.

Let
\mu
be an admissible measure for
\gamma_{\text{{sy}},2}(E)
. Then, by definition,

\int_{E}|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{x})|^{2}\text{d}\mu(\overline{x})\leq\mu(E),\quad\forall\varepsilon>0.

Consider the maximal operator

\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\ast}\mu(\overline{x}):=\sup_{\varepsilon>0}|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{x})|,

that by the nonnegativity of
P_{\text{{sy}}}
and
\mu
is such that

\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\ast}\mu(\overline{x})=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{x}),

which is well-defined for every point
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. In fact, by the monotone convergence theorem, it is clear that the above
L^{2}(\mu)
uniform estimate with respect to
\varepsilon>0
, can be rewritten as

\int_{E}|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\ast}\mu(\overline{x})|^{2}\text{d}\mu(\overline{x})\leq\mu(E).

Now we define

F:=\Big\{\overline{x}\in E\,:\,\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\ast}\mu(\overline{x})\leq\sqrt{2}\Big\},

and apply Chebyshev’s inequality to obtain

\mu(E\setminus{F})\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{E}|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\ast}\mu(\overline{x})|^{2}\text{d}\mu(\overline{x})\leq\frac{\mu(E)}{2}\quad\text{and then}\quad\mu(F)\geq\frac{\mu(E)}{2}.

If we are able to prove

\|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\mu|_{F}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu|_{F})\to L^{2}(\mu|_{F})}\lesssim 1

we will be done, since the latter would mean
\gamma_{\text{sy},\text{op}}(E)\gtrsim\mu(F)\geq\mu(E)/2
, and by the arbitrariness of
\mu
the result would follow.

To proceed, let us observe that
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\ast}\mu
can be understood as a non-decreasing limit of continuous functions. Indeed, we may rewrite, for each
\varepsilon>0
and
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\chi_{\{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|>\varepsilon\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})=:P_{\text{sy},\chi_{\varepsilon}}\ast\mu(\overline{x}),

where
P_{\text{sy},\chi_{\varepsilon}}(\cdot):=P_{\text{{sy}}}(\cdot)\chi_{\{|\cdot|>\varepsilon\}}
. Now, let us fix
\psi
any test function such that
0\leq\psi\leq 1
,
\psi\equiv 0
in
B_{1}(0)
and
\psi\equiv 1
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{B_{2}(0)}
. Assume also that
\|\nabla\psi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Write
\psi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot):=\psi(\cdot/\varepsilon)
and define, for each
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

P_{\text{sy},\psi_{\varepsilon}}\ast\mu(\overline{x}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\psi_{\varepsilon}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\text{d}\mu(\overline{y}).

Observe that since
P_{\text{{sy}}}\geq 0
,

P_{\text{sy},\chi_{2\varepsilon}}\ast\mu(\overline{x})\leq P_{\text{sy},\psi_{\varepsilon}}\ast\mu(\overline{x})\leq P_{\text{sy},\chi_{\varepsilon}}\ast\mu(\overline{x}),\hskip 7.11317pt\forall\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

which implies, by definition,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}P_{\text{sy},\psi_{\varepsilon}}\ast\mu(\overline{x})=\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{x}),\hskip 7.11317pt\forall\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

Now, since
\mu
is in particular a finite measure, it is not difficult to prove using Fatou’s lemma and its reverse version, that for each
\varepsilon>0
fixed,
P_{\text{sy},\psi_{\varepsilon}}\ast\mu
is a continuous function. Moreover, since
\mu
and
P_{\text{{sy}}}
are nonnegative, it is clear that for
\varepsilon_{1}<\varepsilon_{2}
, then
P_{\text{sy},\psi_{\varepsilon_{2}}}\ast\mu\leq P_{\text{sy},\psi_{\varepsilon_{1}}}\ast\mu
. Hence, indeed,
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\ast}\mu
can be defined as a non-decreasing limit of continuous functions. Therefore,
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\ast}\mu
is lower semicontinuous
[
Ba
, Ch.4]
and thus
F
is a closed set. Now consider
B
a large open ball containing
E
inside and define
G:=B\setminus{F}
. For any
\overline{z}\in G\cap E=E\setminus{F}
we define

\varepsilon(\overline{z}):=\text{dist}(\overline{z},F)=\Lambda(\overline{z}),

recalling the notation used when defining the suppressed kernels. First we estimate for any
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon(\overline{z})
:

\displaystyle\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{z})=\int_{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\geq 2\varepsilon}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})+\int_{\varepsilon<|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\leq 2\varepsilon}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})=:\text{I}+\text{II}.

Notice that by the
n
-growth of
\mu
we have

\displaystyle|\text{II}|\leq\frac{\mu(B_{2\varepsilon}(\overline{z}))}{\varepsilon^{n}}\lesssim 1.

To deal with I, let
\overline{x}_{0}
be a closest point to
\overline{z}
on
F
, so that
\varepsilon(\overline{z})=|\overline{x}_{0}-\overline{z}|
. Then

\displaystyle|\text{I}|\leq\int_{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\geq 2\varepsilon}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}_{0}-\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})+\int_{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\geq 2\varepsilon}|P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}_{0}-\overline{y})-P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})=:\text{III}+\text{IV}.

It is clear that
\text{III}\leq\sqrt{2}
, since
\overline{x}_{0}\in F
. On the other hand, since
|(\overline{z}-\overline{y})-(\overline{x}_{0}-\overline{y})|\leq\varepsilon\leq|\overline{z}-\overline{y}|/2
, integration over annuli yields

\displaystyle|\text{IV}|\lesssim\varepsilon\int_{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\geq\varepsilon}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})}{|\overline{z}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}\lesssim 1.

So we have
|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{z})|\lesssim 1
, for any
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon(\overline{z})
. Now we shall prove

A:=\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda,\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{z})-\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{z})\big\rvert\lesssim 1,\quad\forall\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon(\overline{z}),

where
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda,\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{z}):=\int_{|\overline{z}-\overline{y}|>\varepsilon}P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}(\overline{z},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})
. Since
\varepsilon(\overline{z})=\Lambda(\overline{z})
by definition,

\displaystyle A\leq\int_{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\geq\Lambda(\overline{z})}\big\rvert P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}(\overline{z},\overline{y})-P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})\big\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y}).

Using the definition of
P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}
we know

(3.4)

\big\rvert P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}(\overline{z},\overline{y})-P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})\big\rvert\leq P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})\Bigg[\frac{P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})^{2}\Lambda(\overline{z})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}{1+P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})^{2}\Lambda(\overline{z})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}}\Bigg].

In the region of integration
\Lambda(\overline{z})\leq|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|
, then
\Lambda(\overline{y})\leq 2|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|
, by the Lipschitz property. Therefore, returning to (
3.4
) we get

\displaystyle\big\rvert P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}(\overline{z},\overline{y})-P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})\big\rvert\leq P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})

\displaystyle\Big[P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})^{2}\Lambda(\overline{z})^{n}\Lambda(\overline{y})^{n}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})^{2}\Lambda(\overline{z})^{n}\lesssim\frac{\Lambda(\overline{z})^{n}}{|\overline{z}-\overline{y}|^{2n}},

so by the
n
-growth of
\mu
we obtain (again, integrating over annuli)

A\lesssim\int_{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\geq\Lambda(\overline{z})}\frac{\Lambda(\overline{z})^{n}}{|\overline{z}-\overline{y}|^{2n}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})\lesssim 1,

that is what we wanted to prove. Hence, combining this last estimate with
|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{z})|\lesssim 1
, for any
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon(\overline{z})
we deduce

|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda,\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{z})|\lesssim 1,\quad\forall\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon(\overline{z}).

Now, fixing
\eta\in(0,\varepsilon(\overline{z}))
, using property
3
in Lemma
3.1
together with
\eta<\Lambda(\overline{z})
, we get

\bigg\rvert\int_{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\leq\eta}P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}(\overline{z},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert\leq\frac{1}{\Lambda(\overline{z})^{n}}\mu(B_{\eta}(\overline{z}))\leq 1.

All in all, we have proved

\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda,\ast}\mu(\overline{z})\big\rvert\lesssim 1,\quad\forall\overline{z}\in E\setminus{F}.

In fact, this last estimate also holds for
\overline{z}\in F
, since in this case
\Lambda(\overline{z})=0
and
P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}=P_{\text{sy}}
, implying

\displaystyle\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda,\ast}\mu(\overline{z})\big\rvert

\displaystyle=\sup_{\eta>0}\bigg\rvert\int_{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\geq\eta}P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}(\overline{z},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle=\sup_{\eta>0}\bigg\rvert\int_{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|\geq\eta}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert=\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\ast}\mu(\overline{z})\leq\sqrt{2},

by definition of
F
. Hence, we get
\|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda}\mu\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\lesssim 1
, where this estimate has to be understood as
\|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda,\varepsilon}\mu\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\lesssim 1
, uniformly on
\varepsilon>0
. The same estimate also holds for
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda}^{\ast}\mu
, since
P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})=P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}(\overline{y},\overline{x})
, by the symmetry of
P_{\text{{sy}}}
. Moreover, it is also clear that for any cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\big\rvert\big\langle\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda,\mu,\varepsilon}\chi_{Q},\chi_{Q}\big\rangle\big\rvert=\int_{Q}\bigg(\int_{Q\cap\{|\overline{y}-\overline{z}|>\varepsilon\}}

\displaystyle P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}(\overline{z},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{z})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q}\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda,\varepsilon}\mu(\overline{z})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{z})\lesssim\mu(Q),

by the nonnegativity of
P_{\text{sy},\Lambda}
. So bearing in mind Remark Lemma
3.1
, we may apply a suitable
T1
-theorem, namely
[
To1
, Theorem 1.3]
, to deduce
\|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda,\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\lesssim 1
. But for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
supported on
F
one has
\langle\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\Lambda}f,g\rangle=\langle\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy}}f,g\rangle
, meaning that

\|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\mu|_{F}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu|_{F})\to L^{2}(\mu|_{F})}\lesssim 1,

and we are done.
∎

Remark
3.1
.

Due to the above Lemma
3.2
, we may add
\gamma_{\text{sy},2}
to the statement of Theorem
2.1
. In fact, the arguments above also prove that we can also add the following capacity to Theorem
2.1
,

\widetilde{\gamma}_{2}(E):=\sup\big\{\mu(E)\,:\,\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E),\,\|P\ast\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\mu(E)^{1/2},\|P^{\ast}\ast\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\mu(E)^{1/2}\big\}.

3.2.
Normalization by a uniform bound at all points of the support

For a fixed
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
compact set, let us consider now the capacity

\gamma^{\star}_{+}(E):=\sup\big\{\mu(E)\,:\,\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E),\,|P\ast\mu(\overline{x})|\leq 1,\,\forall\overline{x}\in E\big\},

as well as all the variants
\overline{\gamma}^{\star}_{+},\widetilde{\gamma}^{\star}_{+}
and
\gamma^{\star}_{\text{sy},+}
. It is clear that
\gamma^{\star}_{+}(E)\leq\gamma_{+}(E)\approx\gamma_{\text{op}}(E)
. We claim that the following holds:

\gamma_{\text{op}}(E)\lesssim\gamma_{+}^{\star}(E).

Indeed, given
\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
admissible for
\gamma_{\text{op}}(E)
with
\gamma_{\text{op}}(E)\leq 2\mu(E)
, proceeding as in Remark
2.2
we can find a function
h:E\to[0,1]
such that
\int_{E}h\text{d}\mu\geq\mu(E)/2
and
\|\pazocal{P}(h\mu)\|_{\infty}\leq C
, for some
C>0
constant. We know that the latter estimate implies
\|\pazocal{P}(h\mu)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq C^{\prime}
for some other constant
C^{\prime}>0
(by a Cotlar type inequality analogous to that of
[
MaP
, Lemma 5.4]
). Applying now Cotlar’s inequality of
[
To3
, Theorem 2.18]
, for example, we have

\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\varepsilon}(h\mu)(\overline{x})\big\rvert\leq C^{\prime\prime}\big(\widetilde{M}_{\mu}|\pazocal{P}(h\mu)|^{\delta}\big)^{1/\delta}(\overline{x})+C^{\prime\prime\prime}(\widetilde{M}_{\mu}h)(\overline{x}),\quad\forall\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

where

\widetilde{M}_{\mu}h(\overline{x}):=\sup_{r>0}\frac{1}{\mu(B_{3r}(\overline{x}))}\int_{B_{r}(\overline{x})}|h(\overline{y})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y}),

\delta\in(0,1)
is arbitrarily fixed and
C^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime\prime\prime}
are positive constants that depend on the dimension,
\delta
and the
L^{2}(\mu)
-norm of
\pazocal{P}_{\mu}
, that equals 1 by hypothesis. Since
0\leq h\leq 1
, it is clear that
\widetilde{M}_{\mu}h\leq 1
; and since
\|\pazocal{P}(h\mu)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq C^{\prime}
, we deduce for
\delta=1/2

\widetilde{M}_{\mu}|\pazocal{P}(h\mu)|^{1/2}(\overline{x})=\sup_{r>0}\frac{1}{\mu(B_{3r}(\overline{x}))}\int_{B_{r}(\overline{x})}\big\rvert\pazocal{P}(h\mu)(\overline{x})\big\rvert^{1/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})\leq(C^{\prime})^{1/2}.

Therefore, setting
\widetilde{C}:=\max\{1,C^{\prime}C^{\prime\prime}+C^{\prime\prime\prime}\}
we get

\big\rvert\pazocal{P}(\widetilde{C}^{-1}h\mu)(\overline{x})\big\rvert=\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{C}^{-1}h\mu)(\overline{x})\big\rvert\leq 1,\quad\forall\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

So in particular
\widetilde{C}^{-1}h\mu
is an admissible measure (up to a dimensional factor that makes it an
n
-growth measure with constant 1) for
\gamma^{\star}_{+}(E)
. Thus,

\gamma^{\star}_{+}(E)\gtrsim\frac{1}{\widetilde{C}}\int_{E}h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\geq\frac{1}{2\widetilde{C}}\mu(E),

and by the arbitrariness of
\mu
we conclude that
\gamma_{\text{op}}(E)\lesssim\gamma_{+}^{\star}(E)
.

Remark
3.2
.

Therefore, we conclude that the capacity
\gamma_{+}^{\star}
can be added to the statement of Theorem
2.1
. In fact, since the operator
L^{2}(\mu)
-norm of
\pazocal{P}_{\mu}^{\ast}
is the same as that of
\pazocal{P}_{\mu}
, following an analogous argument we may also add
\overline{\gamma}^{\star}_{+}
as well as
\widetilde{\gamma}^{\star}_{+}
. Further, the same arguments can also be followed to compare
\gamma^{\star}_{\text{sy},+}
with
\gamma_{\text{sy},\text{op}}
, allowing us to also consider
\gamma^{\star}_{\text{sy},+}
in Theorem
2.1
.

3.3.
An equivalent variational capacity

We shall consider a construction already presented in the proof of Lemma
3.2
: let
\psi
be a radial test function with
0\leq\psi\leq 1
,
\psi\equiv 0
in
B_{1/2}(0)
,
\psi\equiv 1
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{B_{1}(0)}
and such that
\|\nabla\psi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Fix
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
compact set and
\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
. For each
\tau>0
, write
\psi_{\tau}(\cdot):=\psi(\cdot/\tau)
and define, for each
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
.

\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau}}(f\mu)(\overline{x}):=P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}\ast(f\mu)(\overline{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})f(\overline{y})\text{d}\mu(\overline{y}).

Let us prove, using
[
MPr
, Lemma 2.1]
(also valid for
P^{\ast}
and
P_{\text{{sy}}}
) , that the regularized (continuous) kernel
P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}
defines a CZ convolution operator with constants not depending on
\tau
. It is clear that
|P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x})|\leq|\overline{x}|^{-n}
. Then, by the symmetry of
P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}
, it suffices to check that for any
\overline{x},\overline{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
\overline{x}\neq 0
and
|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|\leq|\overline{x}|/2
,

(3.5)

\big\rvert P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x})-P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert\leq C\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}|^{n+1}},

where
C>0
is a constant independent of
\tau
. To prove this, we distinguish two cases: if
\tau\geq|\overline{x}|/4
,

\displaystyle\big\rvert P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x})-P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\big\rvert P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x})-P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}^{\prime})+\big\rvert\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x})-\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}|^{n+1}}+\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{\tau}\frac{1}{|\overline{x}|^{n}}\lesssim\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}|^{n+1}},

where we have applied
[
MPr
, Lemma 2.1]
and
\|\nabla\psi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. If on the other hand
\tau<|\overline{x}|/4
, by definition of
\psi_{\tau}
we have
\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x})=1
. In addition, by the triangle inequality,

(3.6)

|\overline{x}^{\prime}|\geq\big\rvert|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|-|\overline{x}|\big\rvert

If
||\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|-|\overline{x}||=|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|-|\overline{x}|
, then
|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|\geq|\overline{x}|
, so
|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|\approx|\overline{x}|
. So in this case,

\displaystyle\big\rvert P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x})-P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\big\rvert P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x})-P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}^{\prime})+\big\rvert 1-\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}|^{n+1}}+\frac{2}{|\overline{x}|^{n}}\leq\frac{3|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}|^{n+1}},

and we are done. If on the other hand
||\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|-|\overline{x}||=|\overline{x}|-|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|
, then
|\overline{x}^{\prime}|\geq|\overline{x}|-|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|\geq|\overline{x}|/2
which implies
\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}^{\prime})=1
. Then, in this case,

\displaystyle\big\rvert P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x})-P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert=\big\rvert P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x})-P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\big\rvert\lesssim\frac{|\overline{x}-\overline{x}^{\prime}|}{|\overline{x}|^{n+1}},

and the proof of (
3.5
) is complete.

Let us also observe that for any
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
any
\tau>0
and any
\overline{x}\in E
,

\displaystyle\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\tau}(f\mu)(\overline{x})-\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau}}(f\mu)(\overline{x})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\tau/2<|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|<\tau}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\big\rvert 1-\psi_{\tau}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\big\rvert|f(\overline{y})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{\tau>0}\frac{1}{\tau^{n}}\int_{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|<\tau}|f(\overline{y})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y}).

In particular, choosing
f\equiv 1
, by the
n
-growth of
\mu
we get

(3.7)

\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\tau}\mu(\overline{x})-\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau}}\mu(\overline{x})\big\rvert\lesssim 1,\quad\forall\overline{x}\in E,\,\tau>0.

Let us define the auxiliary capacity, for each
\tau>0
,

\gamma^{\star}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau},+}(E):=\sup\big\{\mu(E)\,:\,\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E),\,|P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}\ast\mu(\overline{x})|\leq 1,\,\forall\overline{x}\in E\big\}.

Lemma 3.3
.

The following estimates hold:

1
.

\limsup\limits_{\tau\to 0}\gamma^{\star}_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},+}(E)\lesssim\gamma^{\star}_{\text{{sy}},+}(E)
.

2
.

\liminf\limits_{\tau\to 0}\gamma^{\star}_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},+}(E)\gtrsim\gamma^{\star}_{\text{{sy}},+}(E)
.

Proof.

We begin by proving
1
. Let
(\tau_{k})_{k}
be a monotonically decreasing sequence to 0, and let
\mu_{k}\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
be admissible for
\gamma^{\star}_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{k}},+}(E)
and such that
\gamma^{\star}_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{k}},+}(E)\leq 2\mu_{k}(E)
. Passing to a subsequence if necessary, assume
\mu_{k}\rightharpoonup\mu_{0}
weakly
[
Ma
, Theorem 1.23]
, i.e.

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}=\int\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0},\quad\forall\varphi\in\pazocal{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}).

It is not hard to prove that
\mu_{0}\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
. Now fix
m>0
integer and
\tau\in[\tau_{m+1},\tau_{m}]
, and also assume
k>m
. By (
3.7
) we get that for
\overline{x}\in E
,

\displaystyle\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau}}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\big\rvert\leq\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau_{k}}}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\big\rvert+\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau}}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})-\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau_{k}}}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\big\rvert\leq 1+C,

for some finite constant
C>0
. Since
\mu_{k}\rightharpoonup\mu_{0}
we obtain
\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau}}\mu_{0}\big\rvert\leq 1+C
on
E
and for each
\tau>0
. In addition, using (
3.7
) again we get

\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\tau}\mu_{0}(\overline{x})\big\rvert\leq 1+2C,\quad\forall\overline{x}\in E,\,\tau>0,

that is
|\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy}}\mu_{0}(\overline{x})\big|\leq 1+2C
for all
\overline{x}\in E
. Thus, by the semicontinuity properties of weak convergence of
[
Ma
, Theorem 1.24]
we get

\displaystyle\gamma^{\star}_{\text{sy},+}(E)\geq\frac{\mu_{0}(E)}{1+2C}\geq\frac{1}{1+2C}\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mu_{k}(E)\gtrsim\limsup_{\tau\to 0}\gamma^{\star}_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{k}},+}(E),

and
1
follows. To prove
2
, take
\mu
admissible for
\gamma^{\star}_{\text{sy},+}(E)
. Using (
3.7
),

\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau}}\mu(\overline{x})\big\rvert\leq 1+C,\quad\forall\overline{x}\in E,\,\tau>0,

implying that
\gamma^{\star}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau},+}(E)\geq\frac{\mu(E)}{1+C}
, and by the arbitrariness of
\mu
we finally deduce

\inf_{\tau>0}\gamma^{\star}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau},+}(E)\gtrsim\gamma^{\star}_{\text{sy},+}(E).

∎

Remark
3.3
.

Let us observe that, for each
\tau>0
, using the regularized (continuous) kernel
P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}
we may also define the rest of corresponding capacities, requiring different normalization conditions over the potentials. In fact, the following chain of estimates holds for any compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
:

\gamma_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau},+}^{\star}(E)\underset{(1)}{\leq}\gamma_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau},2}(E)\underset{(2)}{\lesssim}\gamma_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau},\text{op}}(E)\underset{(3)}{\lesssim}\gamma_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau},+}(E)\underset{(4)}{\leq}\gamma_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau},+}^{\star}(E).

Inequality (1) is trivial. To verify (2) proceed as in the proof of
\gamma_{\text{sy},2}(E)\lesssim\gamma_{\text{sy},\text{op}}(E)
in Lemma
3.2
. The proof of (3) goes as in Remark
2.2
, and it relies on
[
To3
, Theorem 2.16]
and
[
Ch
, Ch.VII, Theorem 23]
. Notice that the independence of the CZ constants of
P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau}
of
\tau
is crucial to ensure the validity of (2) and (3). Finally, (4) holds by the continuity of
\pazocal{P}_{\text{sy},\psi_{\tau}}\mu
for each
\tau>0
, that has already been argued in the proof of Lemma
3.2
. Observe that the previous chain of estimates also holds changing every
\gamma_{\text{sy}}
for
\widetilde{\gamma}
, where we ask the respective normalization conditions in each case for both kernels
P\psi_{\tau}
and
P^{\ast}\psi_{\tau}
.

Therefore, in particular, we shall restate Lemma
3.3
as

1
.

\limsup\limits_{\tau\to 0}\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},2}(E)\lesssim\gamma_{\text{{sy}},2}(E)
,

2
.

\liminf\limits_{\tau\to 0}\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},2}(E)\gtrsim\gamma_{\text{{sy}},2}(E)
.

Now, bearing in mind that we have found positive constants
C_{1},C_{2}
so that

•

\limsup\limits_{\tau\to 0}\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},2}(E)\leq C_{1}\,\gamma_{\text{{sy}},2}(E)
,

•

\gamma_{\text{{sy}},2}(E)\leq C_{2}\,\gamma_{\text{sy},\text{op}}(E)
,

we define the variational capacity as follows:

Definition 3.1
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a compact set and choose
\tau_{0}
small enough so that
\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{0}},2}(E)\leq 2C_{1}C_{2}\,\gamma_{\text{sy},\text{op}}(E).
Let
\pazocal{S}
be the convolution operator associated to the kernel
P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau_{0}}
(that depends on
\tau_{0}
and thus on
E
). We define the
variational capacity of

E
as

\gamma_{\text{var}}(E):=\sup\Bigg\{\frac{\mu(E)^{2}}{\mu(E)+\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}\,:\,\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E)\Bigg\}.

We convey that expressions of the form
\frac{0}{0}
equal 0.

Lemma 3.4
.

The supremum in
\gamma_{\text{{var}}}(E)
is attained. Moreover,
\gamma_{\text{{var}}}(E)\approx\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\text{{op}}}(E)
, where we remark that the implicit constants do not depend on
E
.

Proof.

For each
\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
define

F(\mu):=\frac{\mu(E)^{2}}{\mu(E)+\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}.

As it has already been mentioned in the proof of Lemma
3.3
, it is not difficult to prove that
\Sigma_{n}(E)
is closed and sequentially compact with respect to weak convergence of measures. In fact,
\Sigma_{n}(E)
is compact (since it is contained in the space of finite signed Radon measures on
E
, which is metrizable, thought as the dual of the separable space
\pazocal{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
). Moreover, it is clear that if
\mu_{k}\rightharpoonup\mu_{0}
on
\Sigma_{n}(E)
, then
\mu_{k}(E)\to\mu_{0}(E)
. In this setting, we also claim

(3.8)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{k}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}=\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{0}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}.

To prove the claim (
3.8
) we argue as follows: given continuous functions
\varphi_{i},\psi_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots N
in
\pazocal{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, by definition of weak convergence of measures we get

(3.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varphi_{i}(\overline{x})\psi_{i}(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}\varphi_{i}(\overline{x})\psi_{i}(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}\varphi_{i}(\overline{x})\psi_{i}(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)\bigg(\int_{E}\varphi_{j}(\overline{x})\psi_{j}(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigg(\int_{E}\varphi_{i}(\overline{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\bigg(\int_{E}\psi_{i}^{2}(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 31.2982pt+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\bigg(\int_{E}\varphi_{i}(\overline{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)\bigg(\int_{E}\varphi_{j}(\overline{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)\bigg(\int_{E}\psi_{i}(\overline{y})\psi_{j}(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})\bigg)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varphi_{i}(\overline{x})\psi_{i}(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{y}).

Observe that the collection of continuous functions on the compact set
E\times E
of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varphi_{i}(\overline{x})\psi_{i}(\overline{y})
is an algebra that contains the constant functions (on
E
) and separates points. Therefore, by the Stone-Weierstrass theorem, every continuous function
f:E\times E\to\mathbb{R}
can be uniformly approximated by such sums. Namely, consider

\big(g_{j}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\big)_{j}:=\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{j}}\varphi_{i,j}(\overline{x})\psi_{i,j}(\overline{y})\bigg)_{j},

so that
\|f-g_{j}\|_{\infty}:=\|f-g_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(E\times E)}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. Observe:

\displaystyle\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}f(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})-\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}f(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\rvert\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}f(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})-\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}g_{j}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\bigg\rvert\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}g_{j}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})-\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}g_{j}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\bigg\rvert\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}g_{j}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{y})-\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}f(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=:\text{I}+\text{II}+\text{III}.

Choose
j
large enough so that
\|g_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|f\|_{\infty}
. We estimate I as follows

I

\displaystyle=\Bigg\rvert\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}(f+g_{j})(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)\bigg(\int_{E}(f-g_{j})(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu({\overline{y}})\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 3\|f\|_{\infty}\cdot\text{diam}(E)^{3n}\|f-g_{j}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{j\to\infty}0.

Term III can be treated the same way. For II use that
\mu_{k}\rightharpoonup\mu_{0}
as
k\to\infty
and apply (
3.9
) to deduce that II also tends to 0 as
j\to\infty
. The latter can be done since the supports of the measures
\mu_{k}
are all contained in
E
, so we may understand each factor
\varphi_{i,j}
and
\psi_{i,j}
of the summands that define
g_{j}
once extended continuously onto
\pazocal{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. Hence, by the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
we deduce

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}f(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{k}(\overline{y})=\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}f(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{x})\bigg)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{y}),

for any
f
continuous on
E\times E
. Therefore, applying this result to
f:=P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau_{0}}|_{E}\geq 0
we get (
3.8
). All in all, we have proved that
F
defines a continuous functional on a compact space, meaning that it attains its maximum and thus the supremum that defines
\gamma_{\text{var}}
is indeed attained.

In order to prove
\gamma_{\text{{var}}}(E)\approx\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\text{{op}}}(E)
, we claim that it suffices to prove

\gamma_{\text{{var}}}(E)\approx\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{0}},2}(E).

Indeed, since
0\leq P_{\text{{sy}}}\psi_{\tau_{0}}\leq P_{\text{{sy}}}
, we trivially have
\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\text{{op}}}(E)\leq\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{0}},2}(E)
; and by the choice of
\tau_{0}
, we also have
\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{0}},2}(E)\leq 2C_{1}C_{2}\,\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\text{{op}}}(E)
, so the claim follows.

Further, observe that we can restrict ourselves to the case that there exists
\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
such that
F(\mu)>0
. If this was not the case, since
\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\lesssim\text{diam}(E)^{2n}/\tau_{0}^{n}<\infty
, we would have, necessarily,
\mu(E)=0
, implying, by definition,
\gamma_{\text{var}}(E)=0=\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{0}},2}(E)
.

So let us assume that there exists
\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
such that
F(\mu)>0
, meaning
\gamma_{\text{var}}(E)>0
. Let us begin by proving that any extremal
\mu_{0}
for
F
in this setting satisfies

(3.10)

\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{0}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}\leq\mu_{0}(E).

If this was not the case, then
\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{0}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}=M\mu_{0}(E)
for some
M>1
. Define
\mu_{1}:=M^{-1/2}\mu_{0}\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
and notice that

\displaystyle F(\mu_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{M^{-1}\mu_{0}(E)^{2}}{M^{-1/2}\mu_{0}(E)+\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{1}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}}=\frac{\mu_{0}(E)}{2M^{1/2}}>\frac{\mu_{0}(E)}{1+M}=F(\mu_{0}),

that is a contradiction. Then, (
3.10
) holds, and it implies, by definition,
\mu_{0}(E)\leq\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{0}},2}(E)
. So we have

\gamma_{\text{var}}(E)=F(\mu_{0})<\mu_{0}(E)\leq\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{0}},2}(E).

On the other hand, for any
\mu
admissible for
\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau_{0}},2}(E)
,

\frac{\mu(E)}{2}=\frac{\mu(E)^{2}}{\mu(E)+\mu(E)}\leq\frac{\mu(E)^{2}}{\mu(E)+\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}\leq\gamma_{\text{var}}(E),

and the proof is complete.
∎

Remark
3.4
.

Let us notice that (
3.10
) holds in general for any extremal measure
\mu_{0}
for
F
. Indeed, in the case that for any
\mu\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
one had
F(\mu)=0
, then
\mu(E)=0
. So any measure would be extremal and (
3.10
) would hold trivially.

Finally, we are ready to give an improved version of Theorem
2.1
:

Theorem 3.5
.

For any
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
compact set,

\gamma_{+}(E)\approx\gamma^{\star}_{+}(E),

where the respective bounds of the potentials can be taken indifferently and independently for
P,P^{\ast},P_{\text{{sy}}}
or both
P
and
P^{\ast}
simultaneously. Moreover, all the previous capacities are also comparable to
\gamma_{\text{{sy}},2}(E),\;\gamma_{\text{{op}}}(E),\;\gamma_{\text{{sy,op}}}(E)
and
\gamma_{\text{{var}}}(E)
.

In addition, for each
\tau>0
the following holds

\displaystyle\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},+}(E)

\displaystyle\approx\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},+}^{\star}(E)\approx\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},2}(E)\approx\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},\text{{op}}}(E)

as well as
\limsup_{\tau\to 0}\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},+}(E)\lesssim\gamma_{\text{{sy}},+}(E)\lesssim\liminf_{\tau\to 0}\gamma_{\text{{sy}},\psi_{\tau},+}(E)
. The latter relations for the capacities depending on
\psi_{\tau}
also hold changing each
\gamma_{\text{{sy}}}
for
\widetilde{\gamma}
.

4.
The construction of the cubes

The goal of this section is to use Theorem
3.5
to carry out the construction done in
[
Vo
, §5.2]
for Riesz kernels. The aim of this section is to prove the following result:

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
E\subset B_{1}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a compact set consisting of a finite union of closed cubes, with sides parallel to the axes. Then, there exists a finite collection of dyadic cubes
\{\pazocal{Q}_{1},\ldots,\pazocal{Q}_{N}\}
that cover
E
and such that
\frac{1}{2}\pazocal{Q}_{1},\ldots,\frac{1}{2}\pazocal{Q}_{N}
have disjoint interiors. Moreover, if
\pazocal{F}:=\cup_{i=1}^{N}\pazocal{Q}_{i}
,

\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}}
.

\frac{5}{8}\pazocal{Q}_{i}\cap E\neq\varnothing
, for each
i=1,\ldots,N
.

\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
.

\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(\pazocal{F})\leq C_{0}\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)
.

\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
.

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(2\pazocal{Q}_{i}\cap E)\leq C_{1}\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)
.

\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{4}}
.

If
\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)\leq C_{+}\text{{diam}}(E)^{n}
, then
\text{{diam}}(\pazocal{Q}_{i})\leq\frac{1}{10}\text{{diam}}(E)
, for each
i=1,\ldots,N
.

\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{5}}
.

The family
\{5\pazocal{Q}_{1},\ldots,5\pazocal{Q}_{N}\}
has bounded overlap with constant
C_{2}
.

Letters
C_{0},C_{1},C_{2},C_{+}
refer to constants.

Before proceeding, let us clarify the from this point on
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
will be a fixed compact set and
\mu_{0}
will always denote a maximizer of
\gamma_{\text{var}}(E)
. Let us also write explicitly the following expression, that will appear repeatedly, for the sake of clarity: for
\mu,\nu
say positive finite Borel measures supported on
E
, we have, in light of Definition
3.1
,

\pazocal{S}_{\mu}(\pazocal{S}\nu)(\overline{x})=\int_{E}\int_{E}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{y}-\overline{z})\psi_{\tau_{0}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\psi_{\tau_{0}}(\overline{y}-\overline{z})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{z})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y}).

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
H
be a positive Borel measure supported on
E
such that for any
\lambda_{0}>0
and any
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{0}]
,
\mu_{\lambda}:=\mu_{0}+\lambda H\in\Sigma_{n}(E)
. Then,

H(E)\mu_{0}(E)\bigg(\mu_{0}(E)+2\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{0}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}\bigg)\leq\mu_{0}(E)^{2}\int_{E}\Big(|\pazocal{S}\mu_{0}|^{2}+2\pazocal{S}_{\mu_{0}}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H.

Proof.

Let us begin by noticing that

F(\mu_{\lambda})=\frac{\big[\mu_{0}(E)+\lambda H(E)\big]^{2}}{\mu_{0}(E)+\lambda H(E)+\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{\lambda}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\lambda}},

where the integral in the denominator can be expanded as

\displaystyle\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{\lambda}

\displaystyle|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\lambda}=\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}+\lambda\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H+2\lambda\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})(\pazocal{S}H)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+2\lambda^{2}\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})(\pazocal{S}H)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H+\lambda^{2}\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}H)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}+\lambda^{3}\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}H)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H.

Observe that for
\nu\in\{\mu_{0},H\}
, since
P_{\text{{sy}}}
and
\psi_{\tau_{0}}
are symmetric functions,

\displaystyle\int_{E}\pazocal{S}\mu_{0}

\displaystyle 1(\overline{x})\pazocal{S}H(\overline{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\psi_{\tau_{0}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{y})\bigg)\bigg(\int_{E}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}-\overline{z})\psi_{\tau_{0}}(\overline{x}-\overline{z})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H(\overline{z})\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}\bigg(\int_{E}P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\psi_{\tau_{0}}(\overline{x}-\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(\overline{y})\bigg)P_{\text{{sy}}}(\overline{z}-\overline{x})\psi_{\tau_{0}}(\overline{z}-\overline{x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{x})\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H(\overline{z})

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\pazocal{S}_{\nu}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})(\overline{z})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H(\overline{z}).

Therefore, we may rewrite
\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{\lambda}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\lambda}
as

\displaystyle\int_{E}|\pazocal{S}\mu_{\lambda}

\displaystyle|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\lambda}=\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}+\lambda\bigg[\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H+2\int_{E}\pazocal{S}_{\mu_{0}}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+\lambda^{2}\bigg[\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}H)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}+2\int_{E}\pazocal{S}_{H}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H\bigg]+\lambda^{3}\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}H)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H.

So condition
F(\mu_{0})\geq F(\mu_{\lambda})
can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{0}(E)^{2}}{\mu_{0}(E)+\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\geq\frac{\mu_{0}(E)^{2}+2\lambda\mu_{0}(E)H(E)+\lambda^{2}H(E)^{2}}{\mu_{0}(E)+\lambda H(E)+\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}+\lambda\big[\cdots\big]+\lambda^{2}\big[\cdots\big]+\lambda^{3}\big[\cdots\big]},

or equivalently

\displaystyle\lambda\Bigg\{\mu_{0}(E)^{2}\bigg(-H(E)

\displaystyle+\int_{E}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H+2\int_{E}\pazocal{S}_{\mu_{0}}(\pazocal{S}\mu_{0})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H\bigg)
