\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x}_{0})\big\rvert\leq c_{0}.

Proof.

In
[
MPr
, Lemma 3.5]
, the authors deal with general distributions with
n
-growth. In our statement, however,
\nu
is a specified signed measure and the cubes
Q
satisfy the additional growth condition
\mu(Q)\lesssim\ell(Q)^{n}
. To prove the local integrability of
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)
, let us fix
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and name
I_{Q}:=\{j=1,\ldots,N\,:\,Q\cap B_{j}\neq\varnothing\}
. We compute, bearing in mind Lemma
3.1
:

\displaystyle\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})\big\rvert

\displaystyle=\Bigg\rvert\sum_{j\in I_{Q}}\frac{\langle T_{0},\varphi_{j}\rangle}{\pazocal{L}^{n+1}(B_{j})}\int_{Q\cap B_{j}}P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\varphi(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{y}\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j\in I_{Q}}\frac{1}{r_{j}}\int_{Q\cap B_{j}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{y}}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n}}=:\sum_{j\in I_{Q}}\frac{1}{r_{j}}I_{j}(\overline{x}).

To study
I_{j}(\overline{x})
we split the integral into the domain

D_{1,j}:=Q\cap B_{j}\cap\big\{\overline{y}\,:\,2|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|\geq\text{diam}(B_{j}\cap Q)\big\},

and its complementary
D_{2,j}:=(Q\cap B_{j})\setminus{D_{1}}
. For the first domain of integration we directly have

\int_{D_{1,j}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{y}}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n}}\lesssim\frac{\pazocal{L}^{n+1}(D_{1,j})}{\text{diam}(B_{j}\cap Q)^{n}}\leq\text{diam}(B_{j}\cap Q)\leq r_{j}.

For the second one notice that

D_{2,j}=Q\cap B_{j}\cap\{\overline{y}\,:\,2|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|<\text{diam}(B_{j}\cap Q)\}\subset B_{3\text{diam}(B_{j}\cap Q)}(\overline{x}).

Therefore, considering the annuli
A_{k}:=B_{2^{-k}3\text{diam}(B_{j}\cap Q)}(\overline{x})\setminus{B_{2^{-k-1}3\text{diam}(B_{j}\cap Q)}(\overline{x})}
for
k\geq 0
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{D_{2,j}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{y}}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n}}\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{A_{k}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{y}}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n}}\lesssim\text{diam}(B_{j}\cap Q)\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2^{k}}\leq r_{j}.

Hence

\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})\big\rvert\lesssim|I_{Q}|<N<\infty,

and thus the local integrability of
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)
follows. To prove the second assertion, we use
\mu(Q)\lesssim\ell(Q)^{n}
together with Tonelli’s theorem and Lemma
3.1
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})\big\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\bigg\rvert\int_{F\cap Q}P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\varphi(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{x}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{F\cap Q}\bigg(\int_{Q}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{x}}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n}}\bigg)|\varphi(\overline{y})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})\leq\ell(Q)\int_{F\cap Q}|\varphi(\overline{y})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})

\displaystyle\leq\ell(Q)\mu(Q)\lesssim\ell(Q)^{n+1}=\pazocal{L}^{n+1}(Q),

Therefore,

\frac{1}{\pazocal{L}^{n+1}(\frac{1}{4}Q)}\int_{\frac{1}{4}Q}\big\rvert\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})\big\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\lesssim 1,

and the desired result follows.
∎

With the above lemma, we are able to prove a weaker
localization-type
result for
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}
, analogous to
[
MPr
, Theorem 3.1]
. It will be valid for our particular signed measure
\nu
and cubes contained in
F\setminus{H}
. Recall that the latter inclusion implies, by property
6
in Lemma
5.3
, that for any
\overline{x}\in Q
, if
R(\overline{x})
is the cube centered at
\overline{x}
with side length
\ell(R)
, then
\mu(\lambda R(\overline{x}))\leq\sqrt{n+1}L\,\ell(\lambda R)^{n}\simeq\ell(\lambda R)^{n}
, for any
\lambda>0
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
Q\subset F\setminus{H}
be a cube and
\varphi
a test function with
0\leq\varphi\leq 1
,
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\leq\ell(Q)^{-1}
and such that
\varphi\equiv 1
on
Q
and
\varphi\equiv 0
on
(2Q)^{c}
. Then,

|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})|\lesssim 1,\qquad\forall\overline{x}\in Q.

Proof.

Let us fix any
\overline{x}\in Q
and consider
\overline{x}_{0}\in\frac{1}{2}Q
the point obtained in Lemma
5.14
. Observe that
\varphi(\overline{x})=\varphi(\overline{x}_{0})=1
. We rewrite
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\cdot)
as follows

\displaystyle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{4Q}}P_{\Lambda}(\cdot,\overline{y})(\varphi(\overline{y})-\varphi(\overline{x}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\int_{4Q}P_{\Lambda}(\cdot,\overline{y})(\varphi(\overline{y})-\varphi(\overline{x}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})+\varphi(\overline{x})\int P_{\Lambda}(\cdot,\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y}),

and we apply this decomposition to
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})
and
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x}_{0})
. Therefore

\displaystyle|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})-\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x}_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\rvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{4Q}}(P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})-P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x}_{0},y))(\varphi(\overline{y})-\varphi(\overline{x}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\bigg\rvert\int_{4Q}P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})(\varphi(\overline{y})-\varphi(\overline{x}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert+\bigg\rvert\int_{4Q}P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x}_{0},\overline{y})(\varphi(\overline{y})-\varphi(\overline{x}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\bigg\rvert\varphi(\overline{x})\int P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert+\bigg\rvert\varphi(\overline{x})\int P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x}_{0},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}+I_{5}.

Regarding
I_{4}
, observe that Lemma
5.13
implies

|\varphi(\overline{x})|\bigg\rvert\int P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert=|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}\nu(\overline{x})|\lesssim 1.

The same estimate holds for
I_{5}
. To study
I_{2}
observe that

\bigg\rvert\int_{4Q}P_{\Lambda}(\overline{x},\overline{y})(\varphi(\overline{y})-\varphi(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(\overline{y})\bigg\rvert\leq c_{1}\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\int_{4Q}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n-1}}\lesssim 1,

where we have used that
\overline{x}\in Q\subset F\setminus{H}
, so that we are able to integrate over decreasing annuli using the
n
-growth of
\mu
for cubes centered at
\overline{x}
. The same holds for
I_{3}
because
\overline{x}_{0}\in\frac{1}{2}Q
and
\varphi(\overline{x})=\varphi(\overline{x}_{0})
, so that
I_{3}\lesssim 1
. Finally, for
I_{1}
we apply property
2
of Lemma
3.1
and integrate over increasing annuli to deduce

\displaystyle I_{1}\lesssim 2\ell(Q)\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{4Q}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})}{|\overline{x}-\overline{y}|^{n+1}}\lesssim 1.

Therefore,
|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})|\leq|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})-\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x}_{0})|+|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x}_{0})|\lesssim 1
, and the desired result follows.
∎

The previous lemma implies the main result of this subsection, which concerns a particular weak boundedness property for
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}
. We call it
property 8
, in the sense that it is the additional property needed in Lemma
5.3
in order to apply a
Tb
-theorem.

Corollary 5.16

(
Property 8
)
.

Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a cube contained in
F\setminus H
with
M
-thin boundary. Then,

|\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda,\mu}(\chi_{Q}b),(\chi_{Q}b)\rangle|\leq c_{8}\mu(2Q),

for some
c_{8}>0
constant.

Proof.

Fix a cube
Q\subset F\setminus{H}
. We shall assume that
Q
is open, since the involved measures
\mu
and
\nu
are null on sets of zero Lebesgue measure. Observe that since the center of
Q
does not belong to
H
, in particular we have
\mu(2Q)\lesssim L\ell(2Q)^{n}
. Take a test function
\varphi
with
0\leq\varphi\leq 1
,
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\leq\ell(Q)^{-1}
and such that
\varphi\equiv 1
on
Q
and
\varphi\equiv 0
on
(2Q)^{c}
. Then,

\displaystyle|\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda,\mu}(\chi_{Q}b),(\chi_{Q}b)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda,\mu}(\varphi b),(\chi_{Q}b)\rangle|+|\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda,\mu}((\varphi-\chi_{Q})b),(\chi_{Q}b)\rangle|=:A+B.

To estimate
A
we observe that
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda,\mu}(\varphi b)=\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)
and apply directly Lemma
5.15
to deduce
A\leq c_{1}\int_{Q}|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\varphi\nu)(\overline{x})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{x})\lesssim\mu(Q).

To estimate
B
, set
\varphi_{1}:=(\varphi-\chi_{Q})b
and
\varphi_{2}:=\chi_{Q}b
. Observe that
\text{supp}(\varphi_{1})\subset\overline{2Q}\setminus{Q}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{Q}
and
\text{supp}(\varphi_{2})\subset\overline{Q}
. We now apply
[
To3
, Lemma 5.23]
with
\Omega_{1}:=Q
and
\Omega_{2}:=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{\overline{\Omega_{1}}}
. The proof of the previous result is almost identical in our context: one just needs to change the function
d(\overline{x})^{-1/2}
appearing in the previous reference by
d(\overline{x})^{-n/2}
. In any case, we deduce

B\lesssim\|\varphi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\,\|\varphi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c_{1}\,\mu\big(\overline{2Q}\setminus{Q}\big)^{1/2}\mu\big(\overline{Q}\big)^{1/2}\leq c_{1}\,\mu(2Q),

and we are done.
∎

The previous corollary suffices to prove an analogous
Tb
-theorem to that of
[
To3
, Ch.5]
, since the weak boundedness property needs only to be applied to cubes contained in parallelepipeds
\Delta:=P\cap S
with
P,S
cubes having
M
-thin boundary (and all their dyadic children too) that belong to
\pazocal{D}_{1}:=\pazocal{D}(\overline{w}_{1})
and
\pazocal{D}_{2}:=\pazocal{D}(\overline{w}_{2})
respectively for some
\overline{w}_{1},\overline{w}_{2}\in\Omega
. Moreover,
P\subset F\setminus{(H_{\pazocal{D}_{1}}\cup T_{\pazocal{D}_{1}})}
and
S\subset F\setminus{(H_{\pazocal{D}_{2}}\cup T_{\pazocal{D}_{2}})}
. Therefore, since in particular
H\subset H_{\pazocal{D}_{1}}\cap H_{\pazocal{D}_{2}}
, the cubes contained in
\Delta
do not intersect
H
. Hence, Corollary
5.16
can be applied to such cubes, yielding the following result:

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
\mu
be a positive finite Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
supported on a compact set
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Assume there is a finite measure
\nu
and, for each
\overline{w}\in\Omega
, two subsets
H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})},T_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
consisting of dyadic cubes in
\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})
so that:

1
.

\nu=b\mu
for some
b
with
\|b\|_{\infty}\leq c_{b}
.

2
.

Every ball satisfying
\mu(B_{r})>Lr^{n}
is contained in
\bigcap_{\overline{w}\in\Omega}H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}
.

3
.

If
Q\in\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})
is such that
Q\not\subset T_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}
, then
\mu(Q)\leq c_{T}|\nu(Q)|
.

4
.

\mu\big(H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}\cup T_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}\big)\leq\delta_{0}\mu(F)
, for some
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
.

5
.

If
\pazocal{P}_{\nu,\ast}
and
\pazocal{P}^{\ast}_{\nu,\ast}
denote the maximal convolution operators associated and
P
and
P^{\ast}
respectively with respect to
\nu
, we have for each
\overline{w}\in\Omega
,

\int_{F\setminus{H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}}}\pazocal{P}_{\ast}\nu(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})\leq c_{\ast}\mu(F),\qquad\int_{F\setminus{H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}}}\pazocal{P}^{\ast}_{\ast}\nu(\overline{y})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\overline{y})\leq c_{\ast}\mu(F).

6.

Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a cube contained in
F\setminus{\bigcap_{\overline{w}\in\Omega}H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}}
with
M
-thin boundary, and
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to[0,\infty)
a 1-Lipschitz function satisfying

\Lambda(\overline{x})\geq\text{{dist}}\big(\overline{x},\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{\big(H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}\cup\pazocal{S}\big)}\big),\qquad\forall\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

where
\pazocal{S}
is the exceptional set defined in (
5.13
). Then,

|\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda,\mu}(\chi_{Q}b),(\chi_{Q}b)\rangle|\leq c_{W}\mu(2Q),

where
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}
is the operator associated to the suppressed kernel
P_{\Lambda}
defined in (
3.1
).

Then, there is
G\subset F\setminus{\bigcap_{\overline{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\big(H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}\cup T_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}\big)}
compact, and positive constants
A_{1},A_{2}
and
A_{3}
so that

i
.

\mu(F)\leq A_{1}\mu(G)
,

ii
.

\mu|_{G}(B_{r}(\overline{x}))\leq A_{2}r^{n}
, for every ball
B_{r}(\overline{x})
,

iii
.

\|\pazocal{P}_{\mu|_{G}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu|_{G})\to L^{2}(\mu|_{G})}\leq A_{3}
.

The letters
c_{b},L,c_{T},\delta_{0},c_{\ast},c_{W}
and
M
denote constants.

Remark
5.3
.

Let us give some details on how to prove Theorem
5.17
, although the arguments to follow are just those given in the proofs of
[
To3
, Theorem 5.1]
or
[
Vo
, Theorem 7.1]
, using, essentially, the weak boundedness property instead of the anti-symmetry of the Cauchy and Riesz kernels. Let us recall that we wrote, for
N\geq 4
integer,

\Omega:=\big[-2^{N-1},2^{N-1}\big)^{n+1}.

Fix
\overline{w}\in\Omega
. Recall
F\subset Q^{0}(\overline{w}):=\overline{w}+[-2^{N},2^{N})^{n+1}
. A dyadic cube
Q\in Q^{0}(\overline{w})
with
\mu(Q)\neq 0
is called
terminal
if
Q\subset H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}\cup T_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}
and we write
Q\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{term}}(\overline{w})
. Otherwise is called
transit
and we write
Q\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}(\overline{w})
. With this, one considers a
martingale decomposition
of a function
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
in terms of
Q^{0}(\overline{w})
. For any cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
\mu(Q)\neq 0
one sets

\langle f\rangle_{Q}:=\frac{1}{\mu(Q)}\int_{Q}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu

and defines the operator

\Xi f:=\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}(\overline{w})}}{\langle b\rangle_{Q^{0}(\overline{w})}}b.

It is clear that
\Xi f\in L^{2}(\mu)
if
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
and
\Xi^{2}=\Xi
. Moreover, the definition of
\Xi
does not depend on the choice of
\overline{w}\in\Omega
.

If
Q\in\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})
, the set of at most
2^{n+1}
dyadic children of
Q
whose
\mu
-measure is not null is denoted by
\pazocal{CH}(Q)
. For any
Q\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}(\overline{w})
and
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
we define the function
\Delta_{Q}f
as

\displaystyle\Delta_{Q}f:=\begin{cases}\hskip 30.44466pt0&\text{in }\,\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{\bigcup_{R\in\pazocal{CH}(Q)}R},\\
\Big(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{R}}{\langle b\rangle_{R}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b\rangle_{Q}}\Big)b&\text{in }\,R\,\text{ if }\,R\in\pazocal{CH}(Q)\cap\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}(\overline{w}),\\
\hskip 14.22636ptf-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b\rangle_{Q}}b&\text{in }\,R\,\text{ if }\,R\in\pazocal{CH}(Q)\cap\pazocal{D}^{\text{term}}(\overline{w}).\end{cases}

The fundamental properties of the operators
\Xi
and
\Delta_{Q}
are proved in
[
To3
, Lemmas 5.10, 5.11]
and, essentially, allow to decompose
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
as

f=\Xi f+\sum_{Q\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}}\Delta_{Q}f,

where the sum is unconditionally convergent in
L^{2}(\mu)
and, in addition,

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\approx\|\Xi f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\sum_{Q\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}}\|\Delta_{Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

At this point, one of the fundamental steps of the proof of the
Tb
-theorem consists in using the above decomposition to estimate the
L^{2}(\mu)
norm of the suppressed operator
\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}
when applied to the so called
good functions
. To define them, we need to introduce first
good
and
bad
cubes. Let
\overline{w}_{1},\overline{w}_{2}\in\Omega
and consider
\pazocal{D}_{1}:=\pazocal{D}(\overline{w}_{1})
,
\pazocal{D}_{2}:=\pazocal{D}(\overline{w}_{2})
two dyadic lattices. We consider as in
[
NTVo2
, Definition 6.2]
the parameter

\alpha:=\frac{1}{2(n+1)},

and we will say that
Q\in\pazocal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr}}
is
bad with respect to

\pazocal{D}_{2}
if either

1
.

there is
R\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{2}
such that
\text{dist}(Q,\partial R)\leq\ell(Q)^{\alpha}\ell(R)^{1-\alpha}
and
\ell(R)\geq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
, for some positive integer
m
to be fixed later, or

2
.

there is
R\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{2}
such that
2^{-m}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)\leq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
,
\text{dist}(Q,R)\leq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
and, at least, one of the children of
R
does not have
M
-thin boundary.

If
Q
is not bad, then we say that it is
good with respect to

\pazocal{D}_{2}
. An important property regarding bad cubes is that they do not appear very often in dyadic lattices. More precisely, given
\varepsilon_{b}>0
arbitrarily small, if
m
and
M
are chosen big enough, then for each fixed
Q\in\pazocal{D}_{1}
, the probability that it is bad with respect to
\pazocal{D}_{2}
is not larger than
\varepsilon_{b}
. That is,

\mathbb{P}\big(\big\{\overline{w}_{2}\in\Omega\,:\,Q\in\pazocal{D}_{1}\text{ is bad with respect to }\pazocal{D}(\overline{w}_{2})\big\}\big)\leq\varepsilon_{b}.

In light of the above notions, we say that a function
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
is
\pazocal{D}_{1}
-good with respect to

\pazocal{D}_{2}
if
\Delta_{Q}f=0
for all bad cubes
Q\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{1}
(with respect to
\pazocal{D}_{2}
). Now one proceeds as follows: define the
1
-Lipschitz function

\Lambda_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}(\overline{x}):=\text{dist}\big(\overline{x},\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{W_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}}\big),\qquad\overline{w}\in\Omega.

It satisfies
\Lambda_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}(\overline{x})\geq\text{dist}\big(\overline{x},\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\big(H_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}\cup\pazocal{S}\big)\big)
, so that Lemma
5.13
can be applied to
\Lambda_{\pazocal{D}(\overline{w})}
, and then we have:

Lemma 5.18
.

(
[
To3
, Lemma 5.13]
)

Let
\pazocal{D}_{1}=\pazocal{D}(\overline{w}_{1})
and
\pazocal{D}_{2}=\pazocal{D}(\overline{w}_{2})
with
\overline{w}_{1},\overline{w}_{2}\in\Omega
. Given
\varepsilon>0
, let
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to[\varepsilon,\infty)
be a 1-Lipschitz function such that

\Lambda(\overline{x})\geq\max\big\{\Lambda_{\pazocal{D}_{1}}(\overline{x}),\Lambda_{\pazocal{D}_{2}}(\overline{x})\big\},\qquad\forall\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

Then, if
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
is
\pazocal{D}_{1}
-good with respect to
\pazocal{D}_{2}
and
g\in L^{2}(\mu)
is
\pazocal{D}_{2}
-good with respect to
\pazocal{D}_{1}
,

|\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(f\mu),g\rangle|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where the implicit constant depends on
c_{b},L,c_{T},\delta_{0},c_{\ast},c_{W}
and
\varepsilon_{b}
, but not on
\varepsilon
.

The proof of this result uses the above martingale decomposition of the functions
f,g
, so that one is left to estimate:

\displaystyle\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(f\mu),g\rangle=\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\Xi f\mu),\Xi g\rangle+\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\Xi f\mu),g\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(f\mu),\Xi g\rangle

\displaystyle+\sum_{Q\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{1},R\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{2}}\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}((\Delta_{Q}f)\mu),\Delta_{R}g\rangle.

If our kernel were anti-symmetric, the first term of the right-hand side would be null. Although this is not our case, it can still be estimated as follows (notice that the weak boundedness property will not be used in the arguments below):

\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\Xi f\mu),\Xi g\rangle\leq\|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\Xi f\mu)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Xi g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Observe that
\text{supp}(\mu)\subset F\subset Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{1})\cap Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{2})
and by definition

\Xi g:=\frac{\langle g\rangle_{Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{2})}}{\langle b\rangle_{Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{2})}}b.

This implies, since
Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{2})
is always a transit cube (this is easy to see just arguing by contradiction and using assumption
4
in Theorem
5.17
),

\displaystyle\|\Xi g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu(Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{2}))^{1/2}}{|\nu(Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{2}))|}\bigg(\int_{F}|b|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\bigg)^{1/2}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{T}^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{1}{|\nu(Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{2}))|}\int_{F}|b|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\bigg)^{1/2}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c_{1}c_{T}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Moreover, by Lemma
5.13
and using that
Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{1})
is a transit cube, we deduce

\displaystyle\|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\Xi f\mu)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{1})}|}{|\langle b\rangle_{Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{1})}|}\|\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(b\mu)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c_{\Lambda}\frac{|\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{1})}|}{|\langle b\rangle_{Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{1})}|}\mu(Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{1}))^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\Lambda}c_{T}|\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{1})}|\mu(Q^{0}(\overline{w}_{1}))^{1/2}\leq c_{\Lambda}c_{T}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Hence

|\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\Xi f\mu),\Xi g\rangle|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

The terms
\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(\Xi f\mu),g\rangle
and
\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}(f\mu),\Xi g\rangle
can be estimated similarly (see
[
To3
, p. 155]
), and it is important to notice that to do so one uses Lemma
5.13
for the adjoint suppressed operator
\pazocal{P}^{\ast}_{\Lambda}
. Hence, the only term left is

\sum_{Q\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{1},R\in\pazocal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{2}}\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda}((\Delta_{Q}f)\mu),\Delta_{R}g\rangle.

The above sum is studied in
[
To3
, §5.6, §5.7, §5.8 & §5.9]
, and the arguments can be followed analogously up to
[
To3
, §5.9]
. Obviously, there are changes that need to be done regarding the dimensionality.
Such modifications were already done in a general multidimensional setting in the study carried out in
[
Vo
]
for Riesz kernels. In any case, it is in
[
To3
, §5.9]
where the weak boundedness property needs to be invoked in order to deal with expressions of the form

\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda,\mu}(\chi_{\Delta}b),(\chi_{\Delta}b)\rangle,

where
\Delta
is a certain parallelepiped introduced at the beginning of Subsection
5.4
. Such expressions were already tackled in
[
NTVo2
]
and
[
HyMar
]
, and they can be deduced from the estimate

|\langle\pazocal{P}_{\Lambda,\mu}(\chi_{Q}b),(\chi_{Q}b)\rangle|\lesssim\mu(2Q),

where in our setting we may assume
Q
to be contained in
F\setminus{H}
and with
M
-thin boundary. This precise bound is covered by assumption
6
in the statement of Theorem
5.17
. From this point on, the rest of the argument can be followed as in the remaining sections of
[
To3
, Ch.5]
. Let us also remark that the proof can be followed almost identically (apart from some dimensional changes) taking into account that we have constructed
unique
measures
\mu
and
\nu
so that assumption
5
holds for the operators
\pazocal{P}
and
\pazocal{P}^{\ast}

simultaneously
. This enables to obtain relation (
5.14
), which is essential to carry out the final probabilistic arguments in the proof of the
Tb
-theorem found in
[
To3
, Ch.5]
or
[
Vo
]
.

In any case, in light of Theorem
5.17
and also bearing in mind the argument of (
5.4
) and that assumptions
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}}
and
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
are superfluous, we have proved the following:

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a compact set satisfying
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
. Then,

\widetilde{\gamma}(E)\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E).

6.
The general comparability in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

The main goal of this section is to obtain similar results to Theorem
5.19
but removing assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
. We will be able to do this for any compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

6.1.
The capacity of some parallelepipeds in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

First, let us present some preliminary results that extend that of
[
MPr
, Proposition 6.1]
. Let us remark that throughout the forthcoming discussion, any parallelepiped will have sides parallel to the coordinate axes.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{a\}
be a parallelepiped contained in the affine hyperplane
\{t=a\}
. Then

\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R)\gtrsim\pazocal{H}^{n}(R).

Proof.

Let
\mu:=\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{R}
and pick any
\overline{x}=(x,t)
with
|t-a|>0
. Observe that

\displaystyle P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\frac{|t-a|}{\big[(t-a)^{2}+|x-y|^{2}\big]^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\text{d}\pazocal{H}^{n}(y)=\int_{R-x}\frac{|t-a|}{\big[(t-a)^{2}+|u|^{2}\big]^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\text{d}\pazocal{H}^{n}(u),

where
u:=y-x
and
R-x
denotes a translation of
R
with respect to the vector
(-x,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Let us pick
D_{r_{0}}
an
n
-dimensional ball embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{a\}
, centered at
(0,a)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{a\}
and with radius
r_{0}=r_{0}(\overline{x})
big enough so that
R-x\subset D_{r_{0}}
. Then, there exists a dimensional constant
C>0
so that

\displaystyle P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{D_{r_{0}}}\frac{|t-a|}{\big[(t-a)^{2}+|u|^{2}\big]^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\text{d}\pazocal{H}^{n}(u)=C\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\frac{|t-a|}{\big[(t-a)^{2}+r^{2}\big]^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}r^{n-1}\text{d}r

\displaystyle=C\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|t-a|r_{0}^{-1}}{\big[(t-a)^{2}r_{0}^{-2}+\rho^{2}\big]^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\rho^{n-1}\text{d}\rho.

where in the last step we have introduced the change of variables
r_{0}\rho=r
. Naming
\tau:=|t-a|r_{0}^{-1}
the previous integral can be finally written as

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\tau}{\big[\tau^{2}+\rho^{2}\big]^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\rho^{n-1}\text{d}\rho,

which admits an explicit representation in terms of Gauss’s (or Kummer’s) hypergeometric function (use
[
Bu
, §1.4, eq.(13)]
, for example), obtaining the estimate

\displaystyle P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n}\bigg(\frac{\rho}{\tau}\bigg)^{n}{}_{2}F_{1}\Bigg(\frac{n}{2},\frac{n+1}{2};\frac{n+2}{2};-\bigg(\frac{\rho}{\tau}\bigg)^{2}\Bigg)\Bigg\rvert_{\rho=0}^{\rho=1}

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau^{n}}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\bigg(\frac{n}{2},\frac{n+1}{2};\frac{n+2}{2};-\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau(1+\tau^{2})^{(n-1)/2}}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\bigg(1,\frac{1}{2};\frac{n+2}{2};-\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\bigg).

In the last step we have applied
[
AbS
, Eq.15.3.3]
. This last expression, thought as a function of
\tau>0
, is bounded uniformly on
(0,+\infty)
with respect to a dimensional constant (and in fact attains its maximum when
\tau\to 0
). In other words, we deduce that
P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)\lesssim 1
whenever
|t-a|>0
. On the other hand, it is clear that if
t=a
, then
P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)=0
, meaning that in general

P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)\lesssim 1,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

Therefore we conclude

\gamma_{\text{sy},+}(R)\gtrsim\mu(R)=\pazocal{H}^{n}(R),

and using Theorem
3.5
we obtain the desired result.
∎

The above lemma characterizes the
\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}
capacity of parallelepipeds contained in affine hyperplanes of the form
\{t=a\}
. We will refer to such hyperplanes as
horizontal
hyperplanes. Observe that the above bound combined with
[
MPr
, Lemma 4.1]
implies

\pazocal{H}^{n}(R)\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)\leq\gamma_{+}(R)\leq\gamma(R)\lesssim\pazocal{H}_{\infty}^{n}(R)\leq\pazocal{H}^{n}(R),

for any parallelepiped contained in a horizontal hyperplane. Hence, for such objects,

\pazocal{H}^{n}(R)\approx\gamma_{+}(R)\approx\gamma(R).

Our next goal will be to study the capacity associated to parallelepipeds contained in
vertical
hyperplanes, that is, sets of the form
\{x_{i}=a\}
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
and some
i=1,\ldots,n
. Previous to that, we need to make an auxiliary construction: let us assume that we have a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
contained, for example, in
\{x_{1}=0\}
. Consider
T
a distribution in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
\text{supp}(T)\subseteq E
as well as the maps

\displaystyle\pi:

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n},\hskip 56.9055pt\iota:\mathbb{R}^{n}\longrightarrow\hskip 7.68236pt\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

\displaystyle(x_{1},x)\;\hskip 1.42271pt\longmapsto x\hskip 83.93553ptx\hskip 6.25963pt\longmapsto(0,x)

the canonical projection onto the last
n
coordinates and the canonical inclusion into the hyperplane
\{x_{1}=0\}
. Let us take
\varphi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
a smooth function and define
\beta_{M}
, for some
M>0
(a positive parameter that we may take as large as we need), a smooth bump function in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
that equals 1 in an open
M
-neighborhood of
E
, i.e.
\beta_{M}\equiv 1
in
\pazocal{U}_{M}(E)
. Also, we require that
\beta_{M}\equiv 0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{\pazocal{U}_{2M}(E)}
and
0\leq\beta_{M}\leq 1
. We set

\widetilde{\varphi}:=(\varphi\circ\pi)\cdot\beta_{M},

that is a smooth extension of
\varphi|_{E}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, with compact support contained in
\pazocal{U}_{2M}(E)
. With this, we define the following distribution in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
associated to
T
:

\langle\widetilde{T},\varphi\rangle:=\langle T,\widetilde{\varphi}\rangle,\hskip 14.22636pt\varphi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

which is a definition independent of the choice of
\beta_{M}
, since
\text{supp}(T)\subseteq E
. Observe that
\text{supp}(\widetilde{T})\subseteq\pi(E)
(a compact set of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
) and also that for any
\psi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
,

\langle T,\psi\rangle=\langle\widetilde{T},\psi\circ\iota\rangle.

Notice that the previous identity is accurate because, even though
\psi
and
\psi\circ\iota\circ\pi
may not coincide in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{\{x_{1}=0\}}
, we have
\text{supp}(T)\subseteq E\subset\{x_{1}=0\}
, that ensures the validity of the above equality. Let us take
(\Phi_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
an approximation of the identity in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and set

\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon}:=\widetilde{T}\ast\Phi_{\varepsilon},

for
0<\varepsilon\ll M
, so that the following inclusions hold

\text{supp}(\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon})\subseteq\pazocal{V}_{2\varepsilon}(\pi(E))\subset\pi(\pazocal{U}_{M}(E)),

where the notation
\pazocal{V}
is used to emphasize that we are considering an open neighborhood in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and not
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. So
(\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
defines a collection of signed measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that approximates
\widetilde{T}
in a distributional sense:

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\;\langle\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon},\varphi\rangle=\langle\widetilde{T},\varphi\rangle=\langle T,\widetilde{\varphi}\rangle,\hskip 14.22636pt\varphi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Let us stress (although the reader may have already noticed), that the spaces of functions we have been considering are of the form
\pazocal{C}^{\infty}
and not
\pazocal{C}^{\infty}_{c}
. This can be done since our distributions are compactly supported. Let us proceed by naming
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{\pazocal{V}_{2\varepsilon}(\pi(E))}
, and observe that for any
\psi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})

\displaystyle\langle T,\psi\rangle=\langle\widetilde{T},\psi\circ\iota\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}C\int_{\Sigma_{E}}\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\psi\circ\iota(x)\,\text{d}\pazocal{H}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}C\int_{\Sigma_{E}}\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\pi(0,x)\,\psi(0,x)\,\text{d}\pazocal{H}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\pi(\overline{x})\,\psi(\overline{x})\,\text{d}\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{\iota(\Sigma_{E})}(\overline{x})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\big\langle C(\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\pi)\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{\iota(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})},\psi\big\rangle,

where
\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon}\circ\pi
is smooth and supported on
\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
C>0
is some dimensional constant. Therefore, the previous construction implies the following remark:

Remark
6.1
.

Given a distribution
T
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
supported on
E\subset\{x_{1}=0\}
, there is a family of signed measures
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
with
\text{supp}(\nu_{\varepsilon})\subset\iota(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})=:\iota\big(\overline{\pazocal{V}_{2\varepsilon}(\pi(E))}\big)
of the form

\nu_{\varepsilon}=\psi_{\varepsilon}\cdot\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{\iota(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})},

where
\psi_{\varepsilon}
is a smooth function that satisfies
\psi_{\varepsilon}\circ\iota=\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon}
(a function of
\pazocal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
supported on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
), that is such that

\langle T,\psi\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle\nu_{\varepsilon},\psi\rangle,\hskip 14.22636pt\psi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

The reader may think of the above result as a construction that exploits the fact that the support of
T
is contained in a hyperplane where
x_{1}
is constant, so that we may approximate
T
via an
approximation of the identity with respect to the remaining variables

x_{2},\ldots,x_{n+1}
(here
x_{n+1}=t
). Moreover, notice that if one starts assuming a condition of the form
\|P\ast T\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, by choosing
\varepsilon
small enough we can assume, for example,
\|P\ast\nu_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq 2
. Indeed, just fix any
\psi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
and observe that

\langle P\ast T,\psi\rangle=\langle T,P^{\ast}\ast\psi\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle\nu_{\varepsilon},P^{\ast}\ast\psi\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle P\ast\nu_{\varepsilon},\psi\rangle,

meaning that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}|\langle P\ast\nu_{\varepsilon},\psi\rangle|\leq\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}
, which implies the desired estimate. In the previous argument we have used that
P^{\ast}\ast\psi
is a smooth function, which can be argued thinking of
P^{\ast}
as an
L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
function and thus as a regular distribution. This allows us to prove the following lemma:

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a compact set contained in an affine hyperplane of the form
\{x_{i}=a\}
, for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
i=1,\ldots,n
. Then

\gamma(E)=0.

Proof.

We shall assume that
E
is contained in
\{x_{1}=0\}
for the sake of simplicity. Consider
D_{r_{0}}
an
n
-dimensional ball in
\{x_{1}=0\}
centered at the origin and with radius
r_{0}
. Let us assume that
\gamma(D_{r_{0}})>0
and reach a contradiction. If
\gamma(D_{r_{0}})>0
, there exists a distribution
T
admissible for
\gamma_{\Theta^{1/2}}(D_{r_{0}})
with
|\langle T,1\rangle|>0
. By Remark
6.1
we obtain a family of signed measures that approximate
T
of the form

\nu_{\varepsilon}=\psi_{\varepsilon}\cdot\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{D_{r_{0}+2\varepsilon}},

for some
\psi_{\varepsilon}
smooth, such that
\psi_{\varepsilon}\circ\iota\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\text{supp}(\psi_{\varepsilon}\circ\iota)\subset\pi(D_{r_{0}+2\varepsilon})
. We may also choose
\varepsilon
small enough so that
\|P\ast\nu_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq 2
. Since
|\langle T,1\rangle|>0
, we are able to pick some
\overline{x}_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in D_{r_{0}+2\varepsilon}
such that
|\psi_{\varepsilon}(\overline{x}_{0})|>0
, as well as an
n
-dimensional ball centered at
\overline{x}_{0}
with radius
\eta
satisfying
D_{\eta}(\overline{x}_{0})\subset D_{r_{0}+2\varepsilon}
and
|\psi_{\varepsilon}|\geq A>0
there, for some constant
A>0
(this can be done by the continuity of
\psi_{\varepsilon}\circ\iota
).

Proceed by fixing
Q=Q(\overline{x}_{0})
a cube in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
centered at
\overline{x}_{0}
with
4\ell(Q)\leq\eta
and
\varphi\in\pazocal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
such that
0\leq\varphi\leq 1,\,\varphi|_{Q}\equiv 1,\varphi|_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus{2Q}}\equiv 0
and
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\leq\ell(Q)^{-1}
. Also take
B_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})
a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
centered at
\overline{x}_{0}
with radius
\xi
so that
4\xi\leq\ell(Q)
, and name
D_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0}):=B_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})\cap\{x_{1}=0\}
, that is such that

D_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})\subset Q(\overline{x}_{0})\cap\{x_{1}=0\}\subset D_{\eta}(\overline{x}_{0}).

We finally define the positive measure

\mu^{\prime}:=A\cdot\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{D_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})}

and observe that by the choice of
\varphi
and the non-negativity of
P
, we have

\displaystyle\|P\ast\mu^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\,\big\|P\ast A\,\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{D_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})}\big\|_{\infty}=\big\|P\ast\varphi A\,\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{D_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})}\big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|P\ast\varphi\,\psi_{\varepsilon}\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{D_{r_{0}+2\varepsilon}}\big\|_{\infty}=\|P\ast\varphi\nu_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\lesssim 1,

where the last inequality is due to the localization estimate
[
MPr
, Theorem 3.1]
. Therefore, we have constructed
D_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})
an
n
-dimensional ball that admits a positive measure
\mu
supported on it, proportional to
\pazocal{H}^{n}|_{D_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})}
and with
\|P\ast\mu\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Let us prove that this last condition is not possible. Indeed, for each
\overline{x}=(0,x_{2},\ldots,x_{n},t)\in D_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})
pick an
n
-dimensional ball
D_{\rho}(\overline{x})
centered at
\overline{x}
and with radius
\rho
small enough so that
D_{\rho}(\overline{x})\subset D_{\xi}(\overline{x}_{0})
. We write
\Delta_{\downarrow,\rho}(\overline{x})
its
lower temporal half
, which is obtained from the intersection
D_{\rho}(\overline{x})\cap\{t-s>0\}
; as well as
\Delta_{\uparrow,\rho}(\overline{x})
the
upper temporal half
, obtained from
D_{\rho}(\overline{x})\cap\{t-s<0\}
. Now, integration in (
n
-dimensional) spherical coordinates yields

\displaystyle P\ast\mu(\overline{x})

\displaystyle\geq A\int_{\Delta_{\downarrow,\rho}(\overline{x})}\frac{t-s}{\big[(t-s)^{2}+(x_{2}-y_{2})^{2}+\cdots+(x_{n}-y_{n})^{2}\big]^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\text{d}\pazocal{H}^{n}(y_{2},\ldots,y_{n},s)

\displaystyle\simeq A\int_{\Delta_{\uparrow,\rho}(0)}\frac{\tau}{\big[\tau^{2}+u_{2}^{2}+\cdots+u_{n}^{2}\big]^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\text{d}u_{2}\cdots\text{d}u_{n}\text{d}\tau=A\cdot L\int_{0}^{\rho}\frac{\text{d}r}{r}=+\infty,

where
L
is a positive dimensional constant obtained from integration in the angular domain (notice that
\tau>0
in
\Delta_{\uparrow,\rho}(0)
). So by the arbitrariness of
\overline{x}
we get that
\|P\ast\mu\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}=+\infty
, since
\mu(D_{\eta}(\overline{x}_{0}))\simeq A\,\pazocal{H}^{n}(D_{\eta}(\overline{x}_{0}))>0
. But this is contradictory with
\|P\ast\mu\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, because such condition implies, in particular,
\|P\ast\mu\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\lesssim 1
(use a Cotlar type inequality analogous to that of
[
MaP
, Lemma 5.4]
). Therefore we conclude that
\gamma(D_{r_{0}})=0
for any radius
r_{0}
, implying the desired result, by the monotonicity of
\gamma
.
∎

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a parallelepiped and
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n},\ell_{t}
its side lengths. Let
R_{\uparrow}
denote the upper face of
R

(
contained in a horizontal affine hyperplane
\,)
,

1
\,)

if
\gamma(R_{\uparrow})=0
, then
\gamma(R)=0
,

2
\,)

if
\ell_{t}\leq\min\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}\}
, then
\gamma(R)\approx\gamma_{+}(R_{\uparrow})
.

Proof.

To prove
1
, begin by noticing that
\gamma(R_{\uparrow})=0
implies
\pazocal{H}^{n}(R_{\uparrow})=0
, by the comments made after Lemma
6.1
. Embedding
R_{\uparrow}
into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
via
\pi_{x}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\pi_{x}(\overline{x})=x
, the canonical projection onto the spatial coordinates; we deduce that
Q=\pi_{x}(R)
is such that
\pazocal{L}^{n}(Q)=0
. But
Q
is itself a parallelepiped of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(that we assume to be closed without loss of generality), i.e.

Q=[a_{1},b_{1}]\times\cdots\times[a_{n},b_{n}],\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for some }\;a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\,a_{i}\leq b_{i}.

So there must exist some
[a_{j},b_{j}]
such that
a_{j}=b_{j}
. Hence,

R=[a_{1},b_{1}]\times\cdots\times[a_{j-1},b_{j-1}]\times\{a_{j}\}\times[a_{j+1},b_{j+1}]\times\cdots\times[a_{n+1},b_{n+1}],

and thus
R\subset\{x_{j}=a_{j}\}
, a vertical hyperplane. Applying Lemma
6.2
the result follows.

Moving on to
2
, let us assume
\ell_{t}>0
(if not the result is trivial) and notice that by
[
MPr
, Lemma 4.1]
and Lemma
6.1
we have

\displaystyle\gamma(R)\lesssim\pazocal{H}^{n}_{\infty}(R)\lesssim\bigg[\frac{\ell_{1}}{\ell_{t}}\cdots\frac{\ell_{n}}{\ell_{t}}\cdot 1\bigg]\cdot\ell_{t}^{n}\simeq\pazocal{H}^{n}(R_{\uparrow})\lesssim\gamma_{+}(R_{\uparrow}),

and we are done.
∎

6.2.
General comparability in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

In light of the previous results, we first obtain an estimate analogous to Theorem
5.19
for parallelepipeds, without requiring assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
. Then, we establish a similar result for finite unions of closed cubes of the same size with disjoint interiors. Since
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
can be assumed without loss of generality, the proof of the main theorem follows.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a parallelepiped with sides parallel to the coordinate axes. Then,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}(R)\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R).

Proof.

Let us denote by
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n},\ell_{t}
the side lengths of
R
. Observe that for our purposes, we may assume
R
to be closed without loss of generality. Notice that by Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
we already know the above result if any
\ell_{i}
is null. So let us assume that

(6.1)

\ell_{i}>0,\qquad\text{for each }\;i=1,\ldots,n,t.

Moreover, we will also assume without loss of generality that
R
is contained in
B_{1}(0)
, the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. The argument that follows is inspired by that presented in
[
Vo
, Ch.6]
. Let us begin by applying Theorem
4.1
to the compact set
E:=R
to obtain a first family of cubes
\{\pazocal{Q}_{1},\ldots,\pazocal{Q}_{N_{1}}\}
satisfying properties
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}}
to
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{5}}
. We observe that:

•

Regarding property
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{4}}
, if
\ell_{\text{max}}:=\max\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n},\ell_{t}\}
, we get for each
i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}
,

\text{diam}(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})\leq\frac{1}{10}\text{diam}(R)\leq\frac{\sqrt{n+1}}{10}\ell_{\max}\quad\text{and then}\quad\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})\leq\frac{1}{10}\ell_{\text{max}}.

•

Regarding
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}}
, we get
\frac{5}{8}\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}\cap R\neq\varnothing
for each
i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}
. We wish to study the intersections
2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}\cap R
using the previous property. Notice that
\frac{5}{8}\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}\cap R\neq\varnothing
implies that the sets
2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}\cap R
are parallelepipeds such that their side with maximal length presents length between
\frac{11}{16}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})
and
2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})
. On the other hand, we distinguish two cases for the sides with minimal length, depending on
\ell_{\text{min}}:=\min\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n},\ell_{t}\}
:

A)

If it happens

\ell_{\text{min}}\geq\frac{11}{16}\max_{i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}),

then the parallelepipeds
2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}\cap R
have minimal side length between
\frac{11}{16}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})
and
2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})
.

B)

If on the other hand

\ell_{\text{min}}<\frac{11}{16}\max_{i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}),

the parallelepipeds
2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}\cap R
have minimal side length bigger or equal than the quantity
\min\big\{\ell_{\text{min}},\frac{11}{16}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})\big\}
and smaller or equal than
\min\big\{\ell_{\text{min}},2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})\big\}
, for each
i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}
.

For each
i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}
we call
R_{i_{1}}:=2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}\cap R
and consider two cases

1)

Assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is satisfied for
E:=R
.

2)

Assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is not satisfied for
E:=R
.

If
1)
occurs we are done, by Theorem
5.19
. If
2)
occurs, notice that if
A)
also occurs we get that
R_{1},\ldots R_{N_{1}}
are parallelepipeds such that all of their respective sides have comparable lengths. In other words, there exists a cube
P_{i_{1}}\subset R_{i_{1}}
so that
\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}})\approx\widetilde{\gamma}(P_{i_{1}})
, for each
i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}
. Then, using that
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is not satisfied together with point
2
in Corollary
6.3
, we get

(6.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}(R)<C_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}})

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R_{i_{1}})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}^{-1}C_{1}\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R),

where we have also used property
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
in Theorem
4.1
. So the only case left to study is when
2)
and
B)
happen simultaneously.

In this case we apply again, for each
i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}
, the splitting given by Theorem
4.1
to all the parallelepipeds
R_{i_{1}}
. This way, we obtain a second family
\{\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}1},\ldots,\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}N_{i_{1}}}\}
associated to each
R_{i_{1}}
, satisfying properties
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}}
to
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{5}}
. Let us fix
i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}
and observe that:

•

Regarding property
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{4}}
, now we have for each
i_{2}=1,\ldots,N_{i_{1}}
,

\text{diam}(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}})\leq\frac{1}{10}\text{diam}(R_{i_{1}})\leq\frac{\sqrt{n+1}}{10}\cdot 2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})\quad\text{and then}\quad\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}})\leq\frac{1}{5}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}).

This implies, in particular,

(6.3)

2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}})\leq\frac{2}{5}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}})<\frac{11}{16}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}).

•

Property
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}}
now reads
\frac{5}{8}\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\cap R_{i_{1}}\neq\varnothing
. Therefore,
2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\cap R_{i_{1}}
are now parallelepipeds such that their maximal side length is between
\frac{11}{16}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}})
and
2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}})
(where we have applied (
6.3
)). For their side with minimal length, we distinguish:

A)

If it happens

\ell_{\text{min}}\geq\frac{11}{16}\max_{i_{2}=1,\ldots,N_{i_{1}}}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}}),

then the parallelepipeds
2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\cap R_{i_{1}}
have minimal side length between
\frac{11}{16}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}})
and
2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}})
.

B)

If on the other hand

\ell_{\text{min}}<\frac{11}{16}\max_{i_{2}=1,\ldots,N_{i_{1}}}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}}),

now the parallelepipeds
2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\cap R_{i_{1}}
have minimal side length bigger or equal than
\min\big\{\ell_{\text{min}},\frac{11}{16}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}})\big\}
and smaller or equal than
\min\big\{\ell_{\text{min}},2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}})\big\}
.

For each
i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}
and
i_{2}=1,\ldots,N_{i_{1}}
we call
R_{i_{1}i_{2}}:=2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\cap R_{i_{1}}
. Two cases may occur for each
i_{1}
:

1)

Assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is satisfied for
E:=R_{i_{1}}
, that is

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}})\geq C_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{N_{i_{1}}}\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}i_{2}}).

2)

Assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is not satisfied for
E:=R_{i_{1}}
, that is

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}})<C_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{N_{i_{1}}}\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}i_{2}}).

We are interested in proving
\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}})\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R_{i_{1}})
for every
i_{1}
, since if this is the case, arguing as in (
6.2
) we are done.
The only indices
i_{1}
where we would not be able to deduce
\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}})\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R_{i_{1}})
are, again, those for which
2)
and
B)
occur simultaneously. For such indices
i_{1}
we would again apply the splitting provided by Theorem
4.1
to every
R_{i_{1}1},\ldots,R_{i_{1}N_{i_{1}}}
and construct for each
R_{i_{1}i_{2}}
a third family of cubes
\{\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}1},\ldots,\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}N_{i_{1}i_{2}}}\}
. Now, for each
i_{2}=1,\ldots N_{i_{1}}
one similarly obtains

\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}})\leq\frac{1}{5}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}}),\qquad\forall i_{3}=1,\ldots,N_{i_{1}i_{2}},

and that
2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}\cap R_{i_{1}i_{2}}
are now parallelepipeds such that their maximal side length is between
\frac{11}{16}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}})
and
2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}})
for each
i_{3}=1,\ldots,N_{i_{1}i_{2}}
. Regarding their minimal side lengths we again distinguish cases
A)
and
B)
in the current setting. Finally, for each
i_{2}
one calls
R_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}:=2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}\cap R_{i_{1}i_{2}}
and studies two cases:

1)

If assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is satisfied for
E:=R_{i_{1}i_{2}}
,

2)

or if assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is not satisfied for
E:=R_{i_{1}i_{2}}
.

Combinations
1)A)
,
1)B)
and
2)A)
lead to the estimate
\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}i_{2}})\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R_{i_{1}i_{2}})
. If one obtained such result for every
i_{2}
, proceeding as in (
6.2
) it would yield
\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}})\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R_{i_{1}})
, and we would be done. However,
2)
and
B)
may occur simultaneously for some indices
i_{2}
. In this setting, we would repeat the above splitting argument for the families
R_{i_{1}i_{2}1},\ldots,R_{i_{1}i_{2}N_{i_{1}i_{2}}}
associated to those
R_{i_{1}i_{2}}
where
2)
and
B)
occur.

We repeat this processes iteratively and we notice that after a number of steps large enough, say
S\geq 1
steps,
B)
will no longer be satisfied. This is due to the fact that relation (
6.1
) ensures
\ell_{\text{min}}\geq\kappa>0
, for some positive constant
\kappa
depending only on
R
; and also because the size of the cubes at each step strictly decreases. Indeed, at step
S
of the iteration, property
4
in Theorem
4.1
and the fact that
R_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}}
has maximal side length bounded by
2\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}})
imply

\displaystyle\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S}})\leq\frac{1}{5}\ell(\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}})\leq\cdots\leq\frac{1}{5^{S-1}}\frac{1}{10}\ell_{\text{max}},\qquad\forall i_{S}=1,\ldots,N_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}}.

So choosing
S
large enough, depending on
\kappa
, it is clear that
A)
will be satisfied instead of
B)
. Therefore, whether if
1)
occurs, or if
2)
and
A)
occur, one equally deduces (arguing as in (
6.1
) in the latter setting),

\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}})\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}}).

So tracing back all the steps of the iteration one gets, in general,
\widetilde{\gamma}(R)\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(R)
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
E:=Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}\cup\cdots\cup Q_{N}
be a finite union of closed cubes of equal size with disjoint interiors and sides parallel to the coordinate axes. Then,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}(E)\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E).

Proof.

First, we observe that it is enough to prove the result if
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{N}
are disjoint. Indeed, if they were not, write for each
j=1,\ldots,N
,

Q_{j}:=\prod_{i=1,\ldots,n,t}[a_{ij},b_{ij}]\quad\text{and}\quad m_{ij}:=\frac{b_{ij}-a_{ij}}{2},\quad i=1,\ldots,n,t,

and for each
0<\lambda<1
define the contracted concentric cube

Q_{j}^{\lambda}:=\prod_{i=1,\ldots,n,t}[a_{ij}+\lambda m_{ij},b_{ij}-\lambda m_{ij}],

as well as
E^{\lambda}:=Q_{1}^{\lambda}\cup Q_{2}^{\lambda}\cup\cdots\cup Q_{N}^{\lambda}
. Observe that the family
\{Q_{1}^{\lambda},\dots,Q_{N}^{\lambda}\}
consists of closed disjoint cubes of the same size, and therefore
\widetilde{\gamma}(E^{\lambda})\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E^{\lambda})
. Moreover, by construction we get that for any
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}<1
,
E^{\lambda_{2}}\subset E^{\lambda_{1}}
. Then, by monotonicity

\sup_{0<\lambda<1}\widetilde{\gamma}(E^{\lambda})=\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\widetilde{\gamma}(E^{\lambda})=\widetilde{\gamma}(\mathring{E}),

where
\mathring{E}
denotes the interior of
E
. We have a analogous identity for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}
. Since for our particular choice of
E
we have
\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(\mathring{E})\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)
(see Remark
6.3
), we are done:

\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(\mathring{E})\leq\widetilde{\gamma}(\mathring{E})=\sup_{0<\lambda<1}\widetilde{\gamma}(E^{\lambda})\lesssim\sup_{0<\lambda<1}\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E^{\lambda})\leq\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E).

So we shall assume that
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{N}
are disjoint. In this context, the iterative scheme of the proof given for Lemma
6.4
can be also applied, but now taking into account the parameter

\delta:=\min_{i\neq j}\big\{\text{dist}(Q_{i},Q_{j})\big\}>0.

More precisely, for example, at the first step of the iteration it may happen:

A)

E_{i_{1}}:=2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}}\cap E
has one connected component (and thus it is a parallelepiped) for every
i_{1}=1,\ldots,N_{1}
.

B)

Or there exists an index
i_{1}
such that
E_{i_{1}}
presents more than one connected component.

We would also distinguish whether if:

1)

Assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is satisfied for
E
.

2)

Assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is not satisfied for
E
.

If
1)
happens, we are done. If
2)
happens, observe that if in turn
A)
occurred for every
i_{1}
, applying Lemma
6.4
and the same estimates of (
6.2
) we would also be done. So we are left to study the case where
2)
occurs and there exist indices
i_{1}
so that
E_{i_{1}}
presents more than one connected component. In this setting, we would repeat the above argument for the every compact set
E_{1},\ldots,E_{N_{1}}
, based on the splitting given by Theorem
4.1
. We repeat this process iteratively, that is: at step
S\geq 1
of the iteration we would obtain (for a set of the form
E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}}
(where we convey
E_{i_{0}}:=E
), with multiple connected components consisting of parallelepipeds) a family of cubes
\big\{\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}1},\ldots,\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}N_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}}}\big\}
with diameters comparable to
5^{-S}\text{diam}(E)
. Now we would distinguish the cases

A)

If
E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S}}:=2\pazocal{Q}_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S}}\cap E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}}
has one connected component for every
i_{S}
.

B)

Or if there exists an index
i_{S}
so that
E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S}}
presents more than one connected component.

As well as

1)

Assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is satisfied for
E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}}
.

2)

Assumption
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is not satisfied for
E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}}
.

The possibilities
1)A)
,
1)B)
and
2)A)
are
good
in the sense that lead to the estimate
\widetilde{\gamma}(E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}})\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}})
. If on the other hand
2)
and
B)
occur simultaneously, we would move on to the next step of the iteration, by applying again Theorem
4.1
to each
E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}1},\ldots,E_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}N_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{S-1}}}
. The key point, however, is that for a finite number of steps
S
large enough (depending on
\delta
) we would have
5^{-S}\text{diam}(E)\ll\delta
, so that
B)
can no longer happen, and thus we obtain the desired estimate tracing back all the steps of the iteration as in Lemma
6.4
.
∎

Remark
6.2
.

Observe that constants in Lemmas
6.4
and
6.5
do not depend on
\ell_{\text{min}}
in the first case, nor on
\delta>0
in the second. Such positive quantities, however, do determine the number of steps needed to carry out the iterative argument of the proofs. But this is not an issue, since the constant in
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
is taken to be
C_{1}
and it cancels with its own inverse from property
\text{{{P}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}
in Theorem
4.1
, eliminating possible dependence on
\ell_{\text{min}}
or
\delta
when tracing back each step of the iteration.

Remark
6.3
.

In Lemma
6.5
we have claimed that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(\mathring{E})
, for
E=Q_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Q_{N}
a union of
N
cubes with disjoint interiors. We will give the scheme to prove this by induction. The case
N=1
is clear: first notice that
\widetilde{\gamma}(Q_{1})\lesssim\pazocal{H}^{n}_{\infty}\leq\ell(Q_{1})^{n}
. Now, pick any
0<\lambda<1
and set
Q_{1}^{\lambda}\subset\mathring{Q_{1}}
the closed cube concentric with
Q_{1}
and side length
\lambda\ell(Q_{1})
. Consider
\mu^{\lambda}:=\ell(Q_{1}^{\lambda})^{-1}\pazocal{L}^{n+1}|_{Q_{1}^{\lambda}}
and observe that
P\ast\mu^{\lambda}\lesssim 1
and
P^{\ast}\ast\mu^{\lambda}\lesssim 1
. Then,
\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(Q^{\lambda}_{1})\geq\mu^{\lambda}(Q_{1}^{\lambda})=\ell(Q_{1}^{\lambda})^{n}
, and making
\lambda\to 1
we get
\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(\mathring{Q}_{1})\geq\ell(Q_{1})^{n}
, so the case
N=1
is proved. Now, we proceed by induction assuming the case
N
true and proving the case
N+1
. To do so, we simply use the semi-additivity of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}
(see
[
He
, Proposition 3.1, Theorem 3.11]
) followed by the induction hypothesis:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(Q_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Q_{N+1})

\displaystyle\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(Q_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Q_{N})+\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(Q_{N+1})

\displaystyle\lesssim\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(\mathring{Q_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\mathring{Q}_{N})+\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(\mathring{Q}_{N+1})

\displaystyle\leq 2\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(\mathring{Q_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\mathring{Q}_{N+1})=2\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(\mathring{E}).

From the previous theorem we can finally deduce the main result of this section:

Theorem 6.6
.

For any compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

\widetilde{\gamma}(E)\approx\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}(E).

Proof.

We may assume
\text{{{A}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}}
to hold by the outer regularity of
\gamma_{+}
, that by Theorem
3.5
is a comparable capacity to
\widetilde{\gamma}_{+}
. Then, by a direct application of Lemma
6.5
we are done.
∎

The previous result combined with the fourth statement in
[
He
, Proposition 3.1]
yields:

Theorem 6.7
.

The capacity
\widetilde{\gamma}
is semi-additive. That is, there is an absolute constant
C>0
so that for any
E_{1},E_{2},\ldots
disjoint compact sets of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

\widetilde{\gamma}\bigg(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}E_{j}\bigg)\leq C\,\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{\gamma}(E_{j}).

7.
Further results in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The
\widetilde{\gamma}
capacity of rectangles

In this section we compute the
\widetilde{\gamma}
capacity of a closed rectangle
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with sides parallel to the coordinate axes and respective side lengths
\ell_{x}>0,\,\ell_{t}>0
. More precisely:

Theorem 7.1
.

\widetilde{\gamma}(R)\approx\ell_{t}\,\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{\ell_{t}^{2}}{\ell_{x}^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{\ell_{t}}{\ell_{x}}\arctan\bigg(\frac{\ell_{x}}{\ell_{t}}\bigg)\Bigg]^{-1}.

Proof.

Let
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be such a rectangle and assume, without loss of generality, that its lower left corner coincides with the origin. To simplify the computations, we also normalize its temporal side length
\ell_{t}
to be 1 by dilating
R
by the factor
\lambda:=\ell_{t}^{-1}
. We name the resulting rectangle
R_{0}
, that is such that

\widetilde{\gamma}(R)=\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{0})=\ell_{t}\,\widetilde{\gamma}(R_{0}).

We introduce the parameter
r:=\frac{\ell_{x}}{\ell_{t}}
, that is nothing but the spatial side length of
R_{0}
, as well as the measure
\mu:=\pazocal{L}^{2}|_{R_{0}}
.

By a direct computation, one obtains that the potential
P\ast\mu
at a point
\overline{x}=(x,t)
is given by the following explicit expression:

P\ast\mu(\overline{x})=\begin{cases}\text{if }t\leq 0,\hskip 31.2982pt0,\\
\text{if }t\in(0,1],\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{x}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{t^{2}}{x^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{r-x}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{t^{2}}{(r-x)^{2}}\bigg)\\
\hskip 125.19194pt+\,t\bigg[\frac{\pi}{2}\text{sgn}(x)-\arctan\bigg(\frac{t}{x}\bigg)+\arctan\bigg(\frac{r-x}{t}\bigg)\bigg],\\
\text{if }t>1,\hskip 31.2982pt\frac{x}{2}\ln\bigg[\frac{x^{2}+t^{2}}{x^{2}+(t-1)^{2}}\bigg]+\frac{r-x}{2}\ln\bigg[\frac{(x-r)^{2}+t^{2}}{(x-r)^{2}+(t-1)^{2}}\bigg]\\
\hskip 85.35826pt+\,1\bigg[\arctan\bigg(\frac{r-x}{t-1}\bigg)+\arctan\bigg(\frac{x}{t-1}\bigg)\bigg]\\
\hskip 85.35826pt+\,t\bigg[\arctan\bigg(\frac{t-1}{x}\bigg)-\arctan\bigg(\frac{t}{x}\bigg)\\
\hskip 170.71652pt+\arctan\bigg(\frac{r-x}{t}\bigg)-\arctan\bigg(\frac{r-x}{t-1}\bigg)\bigg].\end{cases}

In the previous formula we have written the factor
1
in the fifth line just to emphasize that such factor would be
\ell_{t}
if
R
was not normalized to be
R_{0}
. It is not difficult to prove that the above expression defines a continuous function in the whole
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(once extended to
x=r
and
x=0
when
t>0
by taking limits). Also, using the following identity

\text{sgn}(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}\bigg[\arctan(x)+\arctan\bigg(\frac{1}{x}\bigg)\bigg],

it follows that for any fixed
t
,
P\ast\mu(x,t)=P\ast\mu(r-x,t),
or in other words, the one variable function
x\mapsto P\ast\mu(x,t)
is symmetric with respect to the point
x=r/2
, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, it is clear that it is nonnegative, tends to 0 as
x\to\pm\infty
and in fact, for
t>0
, it attains its maximum precisely at
x=r/2
. This last property can be argued as follows: begin by fixing
t\in(0,1]
and noticing that

\lim_{x\to 0}P\ast\mu(x,t)=\lim_{x\to r}P\ast\mu(x,t)=\frac{r}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{t^{2}}{r^{2}}\bigg)+t\,\arctan\bigg(\frac{r}{t}\bigg)=:C_{t}.

Compute the derivative with respect to
x
of
P\ast\mu
at points
x\neq 0,x\neq r
, that is given by

\partial_{x}P\ast\mu(x,t)^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\Bigg[\ln\bigg(1+\frac{t^{2}}{x^{2}}\bigg)-\ln\bigg(1+\frac{t^{2}}{(r-x)^{2}}\bigg)\Bigg],

and that satisfies
\partial_{x}P\ast\mu(\cdot,t)<0
in
(r/2,r)\cup(r,\infty)
,
\partial_{x}P\ast\mu(\cdot,t)>0
in
(-\infty,0)\cup(0,r/2)
and
\partial_{x}P\ast\mu(r/2,t)=0
. Therefore,
P\ast\mu(\cdot,t)
may attain its global maximum at
x=0
,
x=r/2
or
x=r
. The value of
P\ast\mu(\cdot,t)
at
x=r/2
is

\displaystyle P\ast\mu\bigg(\frac{r}{2},t\bigg)=\frac{r}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{4t^{2}}{r^{2}}\bigg)+2t\,\arctan\bigg(\frac{r}{2t}\bigg)>C_{t}.

Hence, for
t\in(0,1)
,
P\ast\mu(\cdot,t)
attains its global maximum at
x=r/2
. For
t>1
a similar study can be carried out, yielding the same conclusion.

Now, by restricting
P\ast\mu
to the vertical line
x=r/2
we obtain a nonnegative piece-wise continuous function of
t
given by the expression

P\ast\mu\bigg(\frac{r}{2},t\bigg)=\begin{cases}\text{if }t\leq 0,\hskip 31.2982pt0,\\
\text{if }t\in(0,1],\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{r}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{4t^{2}}{r^{2}}\bigg)+2t\,\arctan\bigg(\frac{r}{2t}\bigg),\\
\text{if }t>1,\hskip 31.2982pt\frac{r}{2}\ln\bigg[\frac{r^{2}+4t^{2}}{r^{2}+4(t-1)^{2}}\bigg]+\,2\arctan\bigg(\frac{r}{2(t-1)}\bigg)\\
\hskip 113.81102pt-\,2t\bigg[\frac{\pi}{2}-\arctan\bigg(\frac{2(t-1)}{r}\bigg)-\arctan\bigg(\frac{r}{2t}\bigg)\Bigg].\end{cases}

which also tends to 0 as
t\to\pm\infty
and it can be proved that it attains its maximum for
t=1
, for any value of
r>0
. Combining the above computations, we have obtained

P\ast\mu(\overline{x})\leq P\ast\mu(r/2,1),\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

For the sake of clarity, Figure
1
depicts the graph of the potential
P\ast\mu
for a particular value of
r
(that already represents its qualitative behavior).

Figure 1.
Graph of
P\ast\mu
for
r=1/2
.

The particular value of
M(r):=P\ast\mu(r/2,1)
provides a lower bound for
\gamma_{+}
:

\gamma_{+}(R_{0})\geq M(r)^{-1}\mu(R_{0})=M(r)^{-1}r=\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{4}{r^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{2}{r}\arctan\bigg(\frac{r}{2}\bigg)\Bigg]^{-1}.

To obtain an upper bound we shall work with the capacity
\gamma_{\text{sy},+}
(comparable to
\gamma_{+}
by Theorem
3.5
). By definition of
P_{\text{sy}}
we get

\displaystyle P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t):=\frac{1}{2}\big(P\ast\mu(x,t)+P^{\ast}\ast\mu(x,t)\big)=\frac{1}{2}\big(P\ast\mu(x,t)+P\ast\mu(x,1-t)\big),

where the last equality can be easily deduced from the definition of
P^{\ast}
and the symmetry of
R
with respect to the horizontal line
t=1/2
. By a similar study to the one done for
P\ast\mu
, it can be proved that
(P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu)|_{R_{0}}
attains its minimum at the vertices of
R_{0}
. Just proceed by fixing
t\in[0,1]
and studying the one variable function
x\mapsto P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)
restricted to the domain
[0,r]
; and by fixing
x\in[0,1]
and studying
t\mapsto P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)
once restricted to
[0,1]
. The former is again symmetric with respect to the point
x=r/2
and attains its minimum for
x=0
and
x=r
, while the latter is symmetric with respect
t=1/2
and attains its minimum for
t=0
and
t=1
. In Figure
2
the reader may visualize the graph of
P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu
for a particular value of
r
.

Therefore, for any
(x,t)\in R_{0}
we have

\displaystyle P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)\geq\lim_{(x,t)\to(0,0)}P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu(x,t)=\frac{r}{4}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{1}{2}\arctan(r)=:\frac{1}{2}m(r).

Now take any admissible measure
\nu
for
\gamma_{\text{sy},+}(R_{0})
and observe that

\langle\nu,1\rangle\leq 2\,m(r)^{-1}\langle\nu,P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu\rangle=2\,m(r)^{-1}\langle P_{\text{sy}}\ast\nu,\mu\rangle\leq 2\,m(r)^{-1}\mu(R_{0})=2\,m(r)^{-1}r,

Figure 2.
Graph of
P_{\text{sy}}\ast\mu
for
r=1/2
.

where we have applied Tonelli’s theorem, the symmetry of
P_{\text{sy}}
and the fact that
\mu\ll\pazocal{L}^{2}
. So by the arbitrariness of
\nu
and the comparability of
\gamma_{+}
with
\gamma_{\text{sy},+}
we deduce that there exists an absolute constant
C>0
so that

\gamma_{+}(R_{0})\leq C\,m(r)^{-1}r=C\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{\arctan(r)}{r}\Bigg]^{-1}.

Finally, using that
M(r)-4m(r)\leq 0
for any
r>0
, we get the following estimate

\gamma_{+}(R_{0})\approx m(r)^{-1}r=\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{\arctan(r)}{r}\Bigg]^{-1}.

Therefore, regarding the original rectangle
R
and applying Theorem
3.5
and Lemma
6.4
, Theorem
7.1
follows.
∎

Remark
7.1
.

Let us check how the above relation extends to cases
\ell_{x}=0
(i.e.
r=0
) and
\ell_{t}=0
(i.e.
r=+\infty
). For the first case, notice that if
r\leq 1/2
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3|\ln{(r)}|}\leq\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{\arctan(r)}{r}\Bigg]^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{|\ln{(r)}|},

where the hypothesis of
r\leq 1/2
is used in the first bound. Therefore, we deduce

\widetilde{\gamma}(R)\approx\ell_{t}\,\bigg\rvert\ln\bigg(\frac{\ell_{x}}{\ell_{t}}\bigg)\bigg\rvert^{-1},\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if}\quad\ell_{x}\leq\ell_{t}/2,

which is a result consistent with the outer regularity of
\gamma_{+}
and the fact that a vertical line segment has null
\gamma
capacity (see
[
MPr
, Proposition 6.1]
). On the other hand, for the regime
r\to+\infty
, the following holds if
r\geq 1

\displaystyle\frac{r}{2}\leq\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\ln\bigg(1+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\bigg)+\frac{\arctan(r)}{r}\Bigg]^{-1}\leq r,

(in fact, the lower bound holds for any
r>0
) so in this case we deduce

\widetilde{\gamma}(R)\approx\ell_{x},\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if}\quad\ell_{x}\geq\ell_{t},

that is what we expected by point
2
in Corollary
6.3
.
